## Chapter 3

## Analyzing Indian Science and Technology Indicators Using Data Science: A Global Perspective

Anant Bhatt^a, Rubani Singla^b, Shilpa Gite^c, Shruti Patil^c and Ketan Kotecha^c

^aFaculty of Technology and Engineering, Parul University, Waghodia, Gujarat, India

^bComputer Science and Engineering Department, Chandigarh Engineering College, Chandigarh Group of Colleges Jhanjeri, Mohali, Punjab, India

^cSymbiosis International University, India

### Abstract

An upsurge in evidence-based policy formulation has led to profound roadmap formulation and effective policy formulation internationally. Significantly, the prevalent national practices in the R&D policy formulation demands painstaking analysis and accountability in investments. Although diverse methodology trends extend feasibility to derive inferences from the figurative assessments of the investments, overall economy and salient growth reports, it is crucial to contemplate the direct and indirect influence of numbers in an eclectic landscape. Mainly, the science, technology, and innovation (STI) indicators are extensively being used to comprehend the evolution of R&D activities. Quantitative science and technology (S&T) data analysis provides valuable and strategic insights for effective policy formulation with effective assimilation of S&T activities, which otherwise is a complex and dreary affair. This paper deliberates on a “Scientometric” approach with deep data science analytics to analyze Indian indicators in consonance with national objectives and vision. We present a detailed analysis of the Indian S&T indicators derived from the published data by credible International/federal agencies compared to the global R&D statistics. The latter part is aimed to curl out the Indian GDP and S&T indicators' pattern and infer the non-quantifiable insights (derived from the dominant indicators) to highlight the effectiveness of the government plans. Since figurative indicators fail to capture process and grey zones, we summarize the salient points that merit attention for robust planning and evaluating socio-economic progress in the Indian context. We focus on contributing towards the indispensable R&D policy foundation, considering a broader, global socio-economic canvas.

*Keywords*: Scientometric analysis; S&T indicators; Indian innovation; Jugaad; Indian research; science and technology

### 1\. Introduction

The present-day economic system is the merchandise of fast-paced globalization, communication technologies, and advances led by information technologies. The global socio-economic landscape is wrought by advanced technological leaps, ensuring the best quality of life. The fleet technological progression since the 1990s impacted the global economy, growth, and standing of the nations. Science, technology, and innovation (STI) positioned themselves at the center of the concept. Hence, generation, exploitation, and diffusion of knowledge are considered a prerequisite to the nation's economic growth and development in the industrial revolutions. The advent of diffusion-driven, digitized information technology improved the ease of generating, constructing, and distilling information to cultivate knowledge. It bridged the voids for creation, nurturing, accumulating, and distributing knowledge, facilitating the developments in Science and Technology (S&T). In addition to providing the befitting environment for invention, diffusion, and commercialization in the S&T domain, the global leaders and stakeholders emphasized creating the valuation from this knowledge ([Lundvall & Borrás, 2006](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib28)). Many nations designed their nation-building roadmap keeping S&T at the center. The emergent attentiveness from financial institutions, Ministries of Finance, Ministries of Education and S&T envisioned generating value from these innovations in the form of productivity, profits, and ultimately, leading the growth, productivity, and competitiveness ([Gertler & Vinodrai, 2005](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib16)).

An upsurge in evidence-based policy formulation led to the profound roadmap and policy formulation internationally. The governments deliberated on collecting data from various indicators to contribute to an enhanced, transparent understanding of prevailing situations. The National Center for Science and Engineering Statistics (NCSES) defined the term “indicator.” The indicator is denied as “A statistical proxy of one or more metrics that allow for an assessment of a condition” ([Martin, 2012](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib30)). Indicators facilitate assessing the prevailing status of any project, program, or activity and enable measuring the progress in scenarios where it is not feasible to conduct direct measurements of the activities. As the indicators extend proximate analytical measurements, they help to outline the differences between pre-activity and post-activity evaluations distinctively, so foresight is often the best presented by the indicators. STI indicators are the most powerful tools for measuring, analyzing, and comparing progress on international standards. They quantify the nation's growth by measuring its knowledge creation, invention, innovation, technological diffusion, and science and engineering talent in a quantifiable manner, which helps comprehend and evolve R&D activities and innovation systems. Since different indicators cover diverse insights, it becomes essential to pick an appropriate one while addressing project-specific requirements. Well-known indicators have been analyzed in consultation with the international methodological manuals, guidelines, and data published periodically by authentic international organizations. Salient indicators having a considerable impact are shown in [Fig. 3.1](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig1).

![image](images/f03-01.jpg)

Fig. 3.1. Impactful Indicators Analyzed by International Organizations for Policy Formulation.

The emergence of S&T indicators led the controlling authorities to realize a national roadmap by ensuring seamless supplement statistics, analytical inferences, and plans for policy designing effectively. The S&T indicators superseded the traditional government statistics as they showed simple quantitative figures with low dimensions. Formal figurative reports refer to the data themselves rather than canvassing the methodology for the data treatment ([Gault, 2018](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib15)). S&T indicators extend information by the patterns, and meaningful information helps shape the plans, thus superintending informed decision-making and policy formulations. Since the evaluation of the key performance indicators can reveal the success and efficiency of the executed management activities, it is imperative to have up-to-date qualitative and quantitative data (supplied by the organisations) to balance the scorecards. Hence, timely surveys, report compilation, and pumping of relevant information by stakeholders to the controlling agencies are also essential.

Developed countries fabricated their policies, conceived the procedures, and aligned the national objectives by converging on the S&T indicators in the early ʹ40s and ʹ50s. The National Science Foundation (NSF), United States, conceived the analogy, followed by preliminary experiments in the ʹ30s and ʹ40s. Organization for Economic and Cooperation Development (OECD) reproduced NSF's concepts in a methodological manual on R&D statistics and formulated the Frascati Manual (1963) ([de la Mothe, 1992](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib8); [Ker & Galindo-Rueda, 2017](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib24)). Publication of the Science Indicators (SI) was done in 1973. The SI and S&T indices were used as a systematic model to analyze the enterprise's capacity and performance with strengths and weaknesses, contributing to achieving the national objectives. Later on, the OECD introduced a series entitled S&T indicators ([Freeman, 2013](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib13)). While the European Report on S&T indicators assisted in identifying growth in the European sub-continent since 1994 ([Delanghe et al., 2011](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib9)), few European nations followed their S&T Indication methods ([Autant-Bernard, 2001](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib1)). Different analytical methods evolved around “Principales Indicatores de Cienciay Tecnologia” in Latin America ([Godin, 2002](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib17)). OECD, WHO, UNESCO, IDB, EUROSTAT, CEPAL, RICYT, and OCTS-OEI are torch-bearers for producing STI indicators with international and national objectives. ICONO(Spain), CTIE, GO- SPIN, OCyT, OCTI, and Department of Science and Technology (DST) STI policy of India focus on national objectives backed by their respective national observatories. A well-conceived STI manual administers each controlling authority. A standardized procedure assures the validity of statistical data to enable international comparison.

The developing countries are at the nascent stage of this cycle. India has considered the S&T indices for economic growth and social transformation since the ʹ50s. Indian policies aspire to accelerate the speed of innovation to deliver science-led solutions. It will help attain sustainable scientific research excellence speedily. For India, the Technology Policy Statement (1883) ([Bhanu & Singh, 2014](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib5)) emphasized the importance of technology competence with self-reliance, which got superimposed by a refined vision, i.e. Science and Technology Policy ([STP, 2015](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib102)), to bring S&T together with a critical factor in R&D investments. STP gives a detailed outline to address national challenges and foster an innovation system. The STI Policy is objected to producing knowledge, generating solutions, building wealth, and acquiring Intellectual Property Rights (IPRs). The National Science and Technology Management Information (NSTMIS), Department of Science and Technology (DST), India, publishes insights on India's R&D indicators which are used in framing evidence-based policies. It helps planning activities in higher education and R&D. The chapter focuses on the Indian approach, mainly analyzing the plans, results, and challenges. It covers the analysis of the Indian policies, their expected implications, and present outcomes. The chapter incorporates a quantitative analysis with a “Scientometric” approach to assess the S&T indicators. The chapter brings forward analysis in the undermentioned structure.

*   To curl out the salient inferences from the global economic landscape and present India's position compared to the leading countries.
*   To put forward the comprehensive and detailed S&T inputs output indicator assessment by converging into the Indian context.
*   We highlight the all-encompassing statistical insinuations of co-relation among various socio-economic factors (including both S&T input and output indicators) concerning the impact on growth.
*   To conduct a quantifiable comparison with the leading countries in the context of the international strategies of the United States, Japan, China, and European countries.
*   Lastly, we bring out salient issues and befitting recommendations meriting attention, formulating Indian policies for improved S&T performance in the Indian subcontinent.

The aforementioned country-specific analysis demands co-relation with the economic backgrounds. Due to the wide variety in the ranking of these countries, the chapter brings out the present standing of the Indian economy and brings out the potential in forthcoming decades from a global perspective using data science.

### 2\. Datasets

The International Monetary Fund (IMF) is instrumental in promoting international financial stability, extending monetary cooperation, and playing a significant role in facilitating international trade, promoting employment, and supporting sustainable economic growth. The IMF facilitates establishing a multilateral system of payments, making the resources available to members. Through its formal system named “Surveillance”, the IMF monitors the financial developments of the member countries. National and global economic and financial development studies help to thwart any crisis in the international monetary system. IMF conducts periodic assessments of global prospects. WEO data incorporates the “Global Financial Stability Report” and external positions of the largest economies in its “External Sector Report.” Though the IMF publishes vital data in diverse fields, we focus on the World Economic Outlook (WEO) database, which publishes various reports on selected macroeconomic data series. IMF data published post-September 2020 highlights the adverse effects caused due to the COVID-19 outbreak on low-income households, impeding the progress in reducing poverty globally with a probability of contraction by −3% at the end of 2020 ([IMF, 2008](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib101)). The base assumption was to see the fading of the pandemic by the end of 2020, which lasted longer than the anticipated period. Since the global economic analysis can be derived from the complete fading away of the pandemic, it is essential to research objective, unbiased, and non-skewed data. Hence, we considered WEO data published in April 2020 that covers the data across the timelines from 1980 to 2020 with medium-term projections for selected indicators. To understand the insights of the Indian context, we focused on the recent data, i.e. Research & Development Statistics published by the Indian Federal agencies ([Research DST, 2019](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib10)). The report summarizes figures collected from more than 6,800 S&T Institutions from various agencies/stakeholders, i.e. government, state governments, public sector, and private sector industry. The survey report covers the national R&D canvas by reporting various input–output S&T Indicators data and sector domain-specific R&D expenditure investments made by the Indian Government enterprises and private sectors. We used the report for deep analysis in the Indian context, derived inferences, and compared it with global statistics.

### 3\. Indian Standing: Global Economic Landscape

To derive a global perspective while keeping the Indian context as a focal point, global figurative data, analysis, and projections of economic developments were explored. WEO database analysis, which incorporates the “Global Financial Stability Report” and “External Sector Report” was carried out. Statistics of the developed nations (with profound S&T practices and outcomes) were used for comparative analysis. [Fig. 3.2](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig2) illustrates the graph of GDP statistics (from 1980 to 2018–2019) for the Indian context. The data are published by the National Statistics Office in fiscal years (April to March). The data incorporate the actual GDP growth rates calculated from 1998 to 2011, published in the [System of National Accounts 2008](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib103). The nation's nominal GDP has shown a significant rise of more than 700% since 1995, with a surprising surge over the past 25 years, where India stood in ninth place in 2010, trailing countries such as Brazil and Italy and fifth in 2019. Growth slowed from 8.3% to 4.0% (between FY17 and FY20) as there were challenges observed in the financial sector and also due to a decline in private consumption growth. The COVID-19 pandemic impacted the global economy. It also affected the Indian economy by a reduction of 7.3% in FY21.

### 4\. Scientometric Analysis

India's approach to development planning has proceeded by routing sizeable resources to S&T, education, training, and research. Substantial results in S&T infrastructure, i.e. national laboratories, research facilities, and academic institutions, have been noticed. India's thoughtful policies and roadmap pumped a reasonable gross expenditure (mainly by the government sector). An analysis derives the R&D association with the economy (GDP), R&D expenditure, S&T human resources involvements, human resource engagements in R&D, and intellectual property registrations. The research papers, publications, trademarks, and patents are the ultimate reflection of the national roadmap. Over the decade, the Indian Government extended additional R&D support through its S&T agencies, and the measures have been supportive significantly. Important factors impacting the S&T paradigm are analyzed in subsequent paragraphs.

![image](images/f03-02.jpg)

Fig. 3.2. Indian GDP Statistics (From 1980 to 2018–2019) With GDP Growth Rates.

#### 4.1 Investments in Research and Development

While the global focus on STI is supported by substantial investments (a whopping $1.2 trillion as of 2009), India's R&D investment figures struggled to make it to a notch (with less than 2.5% of the global investments). Indian R&D investments stayed merely at 1% of the GDP in 2009. However, India envisaged increasing its Gross Expenditure in Research and Development (GERD) to 2% of its GDP and national agenda. Since the industrial R&D investment saw 250% growth with a 200% increase in sales from 2005 to 2010, India envisioned her plans by partying with the private sector. Private investments can transform R&D outcomes into commercial conclusions. By synergizing the rising growth in R&D investments and the part of the private sector, the Indian Government initiated constructive measures to increase its gross budgetary support. The timely actions supported by a sound ecosystem have shown its impact, where India is ranked ninth in scientific publications and 12th in obtaining patents globally. The strength of threads responsible for weaving the “Investment” fabric at the national level is analyzed and compared with global leaders.

##### 4.1.1 GERD: Comparative Analysis

GERD is the total of the current and capital expenditure by the stakeholders, i.e. resident companies, research institutes, universities, and government laboratories agencies in a country. The gross expenditure includes funding from foreign sources for R&D but excludes domestic fund allocation performed outside the purview of the domestic economy. These indicator values are derived concerning the USD constant prices using base years and Purchasing Power Parities (PPPs). They are presented as a percentage of the GDP of the respective countries ([Godin, 2003](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib18)). The report shows that the Indian gross R&D expenditure has risen to quintuple between 2004 and 2018. The GERD shows a consistent rise over the decade from Rs. 24,117.24 crore in 2004–2005 to Rs. 113,825.03 crore in 2017–2018 and Rs 123,847.71 in 2018–2019. [Fig. 3.3](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig3) shows the graphs of the total annual GERD with investments by the government, business enterprises, private sectors and public sectors. The graphs highlight the rise of GERD by a quintuple amount, with a sizeable share from private and public sectors and government funding. The numbers of the Indian GERD on R&D were as expected with the expectancy for its level of development and objectives. However, the business sector's GERD in world-leading countries is reported to be considerably higher than expected for their developments, especially for the USA, China, Japan and Germany. In 2020, India stood in the list of top 50 innovative countries, ranking 48th among 131 countries in the Global Innovation Index (GII). Compared to the leading countries, the Indian Government's contribution is 56% to the GERD, and this proportion is less than 20% percent of the top 10 economies. Significant scope exists to push towards innovation and increase the business sector's contribution to GERD. Since the government funding covered the maximum share of the R&D expenditure before 2004, the Indian Government took corrective measures to make it an all-around development ground. Indian Government successfully drew the interest of the business enterprises and private sectors into R&D investment. The business enterprises' investments rose seven times from Rs. 7,296.84 crore in 2004–2005 to Rs. 51,115.35 crore in 2018–2019, with a 41.3% share in R&D investments.

The figure demonstrates the healthy share by all stakeholders at the national level. Though the private sector showed a rise in expenditure, the GERD share was depleted marginally (from 37.20% in 2014–2015 to 36.79% in 2018–2019). A pertinent vertical of the R&D expenditure is from the higher education sector, which did not exist in the erstwhile Indian framework. [Fig. 3.4](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig4) shows India's sector-wise R&D expenditure with graphs of the national GERD with contribution breakdown from 2004–2005 to 2018. The contribution by higher education has increased seven times, i.e. from Rs 1,254.01 crore in 2004–2005 to an estimated Rs 8,797.08 crore in 2018–2019. While the higher education sectors in OECD countries, Japan, and China account for approximately 20%, 15%, and 12% of the GERD, India seems to be far from the desired standards with mere a seven percent contribution to overall GERD. As higher education and academia act as a significant source of skilled human resources and as a breeding ground for researchers, future policy-making requires attention.

##### 4.1.2 Investments and Measures: Education System

Although the Indian per capita R&D expenditure had increased to PPP $47.2 in 2017–2018 from PPP$ 29.2 in 2007–2008, the R&D expenditure still needs some relook to a budget allotment as Indian figures struggle to make to the notch while comparing to other countries. In comparison to the figures of the GDP spending on R&D among the BRICS countries, i.e. Brazil 1.3%, Russian Federation 1.1%, China 2.1% and South Africa 0.8%, India spent 0.7% of its GDP on R&D in 2017–2018. While considering India's close competitor – China, the R&D spending has doubled in the last two decades. China increased R&D expenditure from 1.46% in 2008 to 2.01 in 2013, resulting in rising numbers of researchers per million. In recent years, South Korea's increased investments in R&D lured a higher number of researchers per million people, i.e. 4,867 in 2008–6,457 researchers in 2013. [Fig. 3.5](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig5) presents comparative charts of the average Indian expenditure, which is shown as (a) in the education system and (b) Indian R&D spending, compared to the other countries. [Fig. 3.6](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig6) shows Indian per capita R&D expenditure (%) in comparison globally. The holistic approach of promoting the R&D demands powering up the education system, industry support and policy raising researchers for a significant connect formulation to invite Foreign Direct. The period can be considered an affirmative signal.

![image](images/f03-05.jpg)

Fig. 3.5. Comparative Analysis of the Indian Expenditure in the Education Sector and R&D Sector. Comparative Charts of the Average Indian Expenditure Are Shown in (a) the Education System and (b) Indian R&D Spending, Compared to the Other Countries.

#### 4.2 Human Resource: R&D

Human resources play a vital role in the growth of S&T. Hence, this indicator also reflects insights for the analysis. There have been numerous relocations of the highly skilled human resources of India. India’s well-trained, IT savvy, experienced people have been relocating abroad for professional reasons. It is essential to identify the human resources in science and technology (HRST) and establish the co-relation concerning the recorded growth. Data on the human resources involved in professional education, i.e. technology, medicine science, engineering, agriculture and veterinary science, education, law and other fields classified under public, private, industrial and institutional sectors were analyzed. [Fig. 3.7](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig7) shows the sector-specific contribution share of public and private sectors in major industry groups. [Fig. 3.8](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig8) shows the comparative analysis: R&D expenditure (in INR) incurred by ministries/departments.

![image](images/f03-06.jpg)

Fig. 3.6. Per Capita R&D Expenditure (%) With Comparative Figurative Analysis.

A substantial rise in the Indian population was recorded from 683 million to 1,000 million within 20 years from 1981. We considered the data on the HRST occupation and HRST education. Although the labor force rose from 306 million to 444 million in the same duration, the core HRST was recorded at 2.6 million and 4. million in 1981 and 1991, respectively. The considerable increase in professional universities/recognized institutions (from 886 to 2,223 in the 1990s) improved the HRSTE figures from 15.6 million to 25.2 million in a single decade ([Khadria, 2004](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib25)). To assimilate the HRST, we analyzed the data of human resources involved in institutional and industrial sectors. A total of human resources employed in R&D activities, auxiliary and administrative activities was recorded at approximately 341,818, 98,824, and 112,327, respectively, by 2018. India stands third after China and the USA in producing doctorate degrees globally, with 16,339 doctorates. However, the global HRST analysis highlights that a comprehensive workforce in India in S&T is low compared to other nations. [Fig. 3.9](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig9) shows Indian standing while comparing the technician's and researchers' involvement globally.

#### 4.3 IPR Regime in India

IPRs give protection to innovativeness and especially to technological innovation. Promotion of the transfer and commercialization of technology aids in improved advancements and stimulates innovations ([Ilie, 2014](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib20); [Mrad, 2017](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib31)). IPRs also promote innovators with assorted incentives, contributing to national S&T developments and economic growth ([Hossain & Lasker, 2010](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib19)). The study showed substantial arguments that strong IPR protection might hinder technology transfer and indigenous learning activities during the initial phases of industrialization ([Kumar, 2003](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib27)). The IPR has proven to be an affirmative factor once the nation has accumulated sufficient indigenous capabilities befitting S&T infrastructure capable of undertaking creative imitation. Similarly, the development experience of India indicates the importance of weak IPR protection in building up local capabilities. Research findings on the Indian context showed the importance of weak IPR protection, which contributes affirmatively. On the other hand, the stronger IPR protection negatively impacts prices, employment and output. However, empirical findings on identifying the IPRs' impact on the Indian subcontinent showed positive, significant implications for the last two decades ([Ezzeddine & Hammami, 2018](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib12); [Mallik & Chowdhury, 2001](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib29)). Hence, the quantifiable R&D outcomes can be analyzed to identify the Indian standing in the said context.

### 5\. Analysis: R&D Outcomes

Acquisition of IPR for innovations and novel work in any domain is considered a prominent indicator of the country's R&D roadmap. S&T indicators take the cognizance of the patent, publication trends for shaping future roadmaps.

#### 5.1 Trends in R&D

Total enrollments in the universities were recorded at 2,431,563 in 1976–1977, 8,399,443 in 2000–2001, and 37,399,388 in 2018–2019, making a significant rise. Significantly, the share of enrollments in professional higher education is recorded at 36.8%. The number of doctorate degrees awarded grew from 16,093 in 2010–2011 to 40,813 in 2017–2018 (a growth rate of 250%). India stood third in producing PhD in Science and Engineering (S&E) globally standing in 2014 and 2015 after the USA (39,710 in 2016) and China (34,440 in 2015).

#### 5.2 Scientific Publications

NSF statistics showed India standing at third place in scientific publications globally, wherein India was ranked at third, fifth and ninth positions for the NSF, SCOPUS and SCI databases, respectively, for the scientific publications. India's SCOPUS and SCI publications growth increased significantly between 2011 and 2016, with 8.4% and 6.4%, against the world average of 1.9% and 3.7%, respectively. Indian scientific publication position slipped to sixth rank in 2016 by recording her share at 5.4%. As the number of publications increased for the USA and China post-2016, the Indian statistics indicate a few grey areas to be addressed. Though India holds a significant strength in human resources, the gap in yearly publication with the top-ranking nations is considerably broader and needs to be filled in. [Fig. 3.10](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig10) (a) shows the Indian growth rate for scientific and technical journal publications from 2001 onwards, compared to the leading countries. While India contributed at 6.8% (with 683,570 publications) to the world's total publications across all fields during 2011–2016, its contribution in science (22 major science disciplines) stays at only 4.1% and CAGR of 6.4%, with 336,620 publications during 2011–2016.

![image](images/f03-10.jpg)

Fig. 3.10. Indian Growth Rate in Comparison to China and USA in (a) the Scientific and Technical Journal's Publication From 2001 Onwards and (b) Patent Applications From 1980 Onwards.

#### 5.3 Patents

Patenting is also drawing attention as it is a measure of novel IPR that measures a country's R&D capability. The patenting figures are derived from residents and non-resident Indians. In addition, the foreign stakeholders also file for obtaining the IPR in India. The number of patent applications has risen sextuple from 8,503 in 2000–2001 to 47,854 patent applications in the year 2017–2018 ([Fig. 3.10](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig10)(b)). Although India is ranked ninth position (in resident patent filing), the numbers are not motivating considering the global landscape. WIPO reported India's Patent Office standing in seventh position in the world. Out of these 47,854 patent applications, only 15,550 (32%) were filed by Indian residents, and foreign residents filed a balance of 32,304(68%). [Fig. 3.11](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig11)(a) shows the graphs for the number of applications made, applications to be examined and applications finally examined. The average number of patent applications filed annually was 16,518 from 1976 to 2018; only 155,566 patents have been sealed in this duration, amounting to 3,795 patents annually. [Fig. 3.11](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig11)(b) shows the positive growth rate of patent applications by the resident Indians from 2000–2001 to 2017–2018 compared to the non-residents' contributions. Out of the 155,566 sealed patents (from 1976 to 2018), only 21.75% of patents belong to resident Indians. [Fig. 3.12](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig12) shows the statistics of the patent application made vis-a-vis the sealed patents with the share of the Indian residents and non-residents since 1976. A pertinent pattern merged out of the figure highlights a numerical contrast in comparison to the leading countries where the share by the residents is higher, i.e. China (90%), Japan (81%), and South Korea (77%).

![image](images/f03-11p1.jpg)![image](images/f03-11p2.jpg)

Fig. 3.11. The Figure Shows Statistics in the Patent Acquisition (a) Quantitative Analysis of the Applications Made, Applications to Be Examined, and Applications Finally Examined Since 1977 and (b) the Share of the Indian Residents and Non-residents Since 2000.

The graphs highlight the increasing share of foreign firms filing patents in India. An enormous chunk of the patent applications by foreign residents, with 18,179 (56%), 4,487 (14%), and 2,773 (8%), are contributed by the USA, Japan, and Germany. While considering the corporate players, [Fig. 3.13](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#fig13) shows Indian statistics highlighting the contributions of Indian residents and non-residents since 1981. ### 6\. Imperative Analysis

International reports published by the survey conducting agencies help measure the nation's effectiveness of the S&T policy by widely accepted concepts, guidelines, and surveys globally. Though the Indian STI reforms' effectiveness can be analyzed by the R&D, patents, and publications output, these conventional indicator matrices record innovations from the formalized sectors only. The Indian survey conducted at the identified first or second-tiered institutions and agencies fails to cover the innovations and advancements in the informal sectors ([Charmes et al., 2016](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib7)). Sizeable hidden indicators do not make it to the surface as most of the Indian workforce is still engaged in the informal economy ([Kraemer-Mbula & Wunsch-Vincent, 2016](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib26)), contributing to a significant chunk of the nation's GDP. In addition, many non-quantifiable aspects do not get addressed and absorbed in these conventional measurements. Knowledge and sector-specific best practices at an operational level are aspects of innovation that are not noticed in any survey and traditional matrices ([Bonnet et al., 2019](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib6)). Lack of identifying such innovations, measures for standard connect, and official medium to nurture these skills make it challenging to capture the workers' in-house knowledge, informal networks, and ability to adapt or imitate formal network knowledge as per their requirements ([Basole, 2014](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib2)). Though the National Innovation Foundation (NIF) identified more than 300,000 innovations, many such best practices are yet to be unearthed. On the other side, the institutions exposed to the procedures showed many patents, publications, and innovations with very little quotients of practical utility. Several instances brought out that the Indian S&T policy prioritized R&D for developing innovative solutions. However, the innovations from rural areas and unorganized sectors were ignored, thus ignoring the prevailing innovations and inherited knowledge.

On the one side, the Indian Government promotes grassroots innovations, frugal innovations called Jugaad, generally belonging to the informal sector ([Sharma & Kumar, 2019](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib35); [Sheikh, 2014](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib36)), and the lack of where-with-all to formalize these innovations for registration ([Bhaduri & Kumar, 2011](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib4); [Jain & Verloop, 2012](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib23)). Since these innovations are constrained to specific problems and realized with local resources, they lack patenting novelty with immediate sophisticated utility. The meager incentive structure and payoff of such innovations do not extend motivation to innovate further. Hence, conventional S&T indicators do not record these innovations. Integrating frugal innovations into formal S&T indicators encounters a significant hurdle. As the patenting procedure is absent in the informal sector, difficulties are being observed in verifying the frugal innovations for their applicability and authenticity. There exists a challenge to ascertain their application on a larger scale. Indian IPR agencies and conventional registration methods have not reached the informal sectors and thus have missed the count of all such innovations, failing to acknowledge them. Cohesively, the traditional innovation metrics leave out the genuine count of the innovations ([Shekar & Paily, 2019](#978-1-83608-298-920251003.xhtml#bib37)). The Indian industry-specific investment figures do not translate to the befitting outputs in S&T indicators. Though the government focus remains in the sectors, i.e. defence, mining, fuels and engineering, the private sector investments are relatively low. The private players emphasized prioritizing pharmaceutical, FMCG, biotechnology, agriculture, etc. sectors where government share is negligible, and the IPR statistics fail to cover any strong inferences. Although the public and private partnerships have helped informal innovations to be on board, “Indian Jugaad” – ground level – innovations are not reaching into formal sectors. It is highly recommended to build “Innovation Hubs” in rural areas to promote and streamline the IPR processes. Making amendments to existing government policies would definitely help advocate, govern and align the informal sectors with the formal sectors of innovations.

### 7\. Conclusion

The STIP indicators are largely limited to R&D support, i.e. government and private player investments in R&D and measuring the quantifiable outcomes in patents, trademarks and research publications. Innovation must be considered beyond the enterprise-level incentives. It certainly impacts the increased revenues and market share. The entire issue of the S&T assessment highlights a potential that needs to be unearthed. The latest programs run by the Indian Government, i.e., “Make in India” Atma Nirbhar Bharat, are improving the innovative capacity. These programs have attracted sizeable traction among innovators, SMEs, private players and institutions. Hence, there is a need to identify indicators that can map the latest innovation policy, which can directly measure policy investment, newly formulated policies, their effectiveness, resulting impacts and economic returns. In addition, it is essential to add a facet to the conventional metrics that can cover the non-quantifiable, unexplored aspects to reflect a correct picture of innovativeness. Since the S&T activities tend to cluster around established or leading actors and geographical locations, conventional methods are not effective in analyzing the changing landscape in STI. India needs to devise an indicator to map and track innovative clusters in the subcontinent. The comprehensive revamp of the entire system demands de-novo look to promote innovativeness. The very basis of the education system must promote research with complete re-orientation of the youth. The higher education system, colleges and academic institutions must form an ecosystem that facilitates a smooth transition from students to researchers and provides platforms to reach out to the NIF to obtain IPR for innovations. There is a strong need to provide conducive environments with good infrastructure, extend appropriate incentives, and nurture and keep the talent within the country. Measures to groom students and scholars require a relook, which can increase potential national multifold. There is a requirement to explore innovativeness and make it reach the utility sector by fusing academia, private sectors, and government agencies' potential to explore new heights in science, technology, and innovations. An ecosystem at the central level can synchronize the objectives, harmonize the efforts of the stakeholders, and bridge the gaps for speedier growth. A focused and well-planned expenditure in the higher education sector, linked with thoughtful planning in R&D, will accrue better outcomes.

## Chapter 4

## Unveiling the Enigma of Shadow: Ethical Difficulties in the Field of AI

S. Catherine^a, N.V. Suresh^b, T. Mangaiyarkarasi^c and Leena Jenefa^d

^aSaveetha Engineering College, India

^bASET College of Science and Technology, India

^cSRM Institute of Science and Technology, India

^dHindustan Institute of Technology and Science, India

### Abstract

AI in the workplace is a double-edged sword. It's powerful for progress but also risky. Well-managed Shadow AI, with a positive outlook, can unlock new levels of productivity, knowledge and a competitive risk associated with all AI outside strict IT rules is Shadow AI. Shadow AI often starts with well-meaning employees who just want to solve problems and work better with the help of AI technology. They're not trying to break the rules, just find solutions. The potential benefits and risks of Shadow AI usage in organisations and their data privacy along with the data classification for secure Generative AI (GenAI) use were discussed with extensive review of literature and suggestions given. Shadow AI, the unauthorised use of AI tools within organisations, presents both opportunities and challenges. This work proposes a conceptual framework to help organisations navigate this complex landscape. The framework emphasises the importance of shadow AI for secure GenAI use, the development of an AI acceptable use policy and the implementation of employee training programs on responsible AI practices. Additionally, it highlights the need for robust security measures to monitor and control AI usage. By adopting this framework, organisations can mitigate the risks associated with Shadow AI, such as data breaches, legal issues and wasted resources while unlocking its potential benefits like enhanced decision-making, increased productivity and fostered innovation. The framework contributes to a future where AI adoption aligns with ethical principles, transparency and the greater good.

*Keywords*: Generative AI (GenAI); shadow AI practices; LLM capabilities; critical thinking and creativity; human connection; authenticity

### 1\. Introduction

The accelerated advancement of artificial intelligence (AI) has presented businesses worldwide with game-changing prospects. But this technological progress also brings with it a new threat: Shadow AI. Organisations face serious risks from this phenomenon, which involves using AI tools and systems without official approval. These risks include data mismanagement, security vulnerabilities and non-compliance with regulations. This article explores the significance of improving transparency and encouraging innovation in a responsible manner. The way we work and live has been completely changed by AI, and its applications are developing at a rate that has never been seen before. According to a recent Salesforce study, 55% of workers use AI in their jobs without receiving formal permission, and about 70% of workers lack proper training on how to use AI safely and ethically. Amidst discussions concerning AI governance and ethics, particularly in the workplace, a novel idea known as ‘Shadow AI’ has surfaced. It frequently refers to AI initiatives or systems created inside of businesses without the express consent or supervision of IT departments ([Catherine et al., 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib7)). These can be as simple as a group analysing corporate data with an unapproved machine learning tool or as complex as an AI model developed and implemented to automate a task. Shadow AI can present opportunities as well as challenges for organisations, so it's critical that they have policies in place to properly manage AI use. The issues of ethics and transparency in the creation, application of AI and hidden AI (Shadow AI). Positive developments are starting to emerge. AI systems are becoming less like opaque black boxes and more intelligible, thanks to explainable AI (XAI).

A growing number of organisations are prioritising ethical AI practices. By emphasising equity, accepting accountability for AI's actions and maintaining transparency, businesses can foster trust and avert issues that may arise from AI that is hidden. Ensuring seamless human–AI collaboration is a crucial component. We can add safeguards against potential risks that might arise with hidden AI by having people involved in oversight, providing input and being able to intervene when necessary ([Silic & Back, 2014](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib22)). Professionals make extensive use of Shadow IT software, which increases productivity and facilitates quicker, more effective teamwork and communication. Furthermore, workers think their actions are just the result of simple ignorance and that they are not doing anything wrong. Although there is a significant increase in IT risks in the context of Shadow IT, companies can still reduce these risks and better safeguard their corporate assets by implementing controls and countermeasures. If companies wish to keep up with the constant advancements in technology, they will need to reconsider their Shadow IT strategies and gain an improved comprehension of how to handle its usage. Limitation is an effective preventative measure, but it is not a cure-all for Shadow IT issues that present opportunities for the whole ecosystem of the organisation. These are the network applications used in any organisations: Greynet is an application installed on the internet by end users that get around security measures to access the network. Content apps (PDF/Extranet) used as an app for content creation and viewing are called content apps. In addition to extranet apps – which are external computer networks that are capable of being accessed from the outside in a controlled manner – they are primarily PDF apps. Utility tools are like a various tool that employees use (e.g. video converters, codecs and PC optimisation and cleaning utilities).

### 2\. Literature Review

#### 2.1 Risks Associated With Shadow AI

Shadow AI's impetuous behaviour carries significant risks, but with appropriate documentation and approval processes, it can also simplify its decision-making process. Unauthorised AI tool use puts data privacy at major risk. When working with sensitive data, security protocols must always be adhered to. An abrupt rise in unregulated AI solutions could lead to unpredictable labour demands and skill requirements. By the proper channels, enterprises can regain governance over these systems. To reduce the risks connected with shadow AI, organisations need to develop a framework for moral AI usage that is in line with their beliefs and policies. This can be done by putting ethical rules and regulations in place. This ensures that any AI abides by rules that protect the company and its customers from unethical AI practices. Any AI system ought to be transparent about its workings and give non-experts a rationale for the choices it makes ([Zhan et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib30)). By demystifying AI processes and making them accessible to all stakeholders, this fosters confidence in AI applications. In order to effectively manage and mitigate the risks associated with Shadow AI, it is imperative that this step be taken to ensure that all AI initiatives have a clear line of oversight. By putting these strategies into practice, the dangers associated with Shadow AI are diminished, and a safe and regulated environment that fosters innovation and experimentation is established. Organisations that recognise AI's potential and guide it through structured pathways can leverage its power to spur innovation while maintaining control and accountability.

##### 2.1.1 Potential Benefits of Shadow AI

Organisations can become more innovative, efficient and competitive by embracing the potential of AI and turning Shadow AI projects into growth engines ([Zhan et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib30)).

##### 2.1.2 Boost Decision-Making Processes

Businesses can take advantage of a variety of viewpoints and areas of expertise to make better decisions by providing AI tools to various business divisions ([Pierce, 2008](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib17), pp. 79–99). Decisions made with this decentralised approach are more effective because it incorporates perspectives from different parts of the organisation.

##### 2.1.3 Boost Productivity and Efficiency

Shadow AI projects frequently begin as attempts to simplify workflows that may result in industry-accepted best practices ([Orton-Johnson & Prior, 2013](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib15)). When workers use AI to complete tasks more quickly and intelligently, the entire organisation can implement these innovations, increasing productivity.

##### 2.1.4 Encourage Creativity and Innovation

Letting staff members ([Milmo, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib14)) play around with AI can help to create a more innovative work atmosphere and lead to the discovery of fresh solutions for everyday issues. Innovations that might not arise from top-down orders can result from this. Many business users are experimenting with generative AI (GenAI) apps like ChatGPT and Google Bard to ([Cui et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib10)) check with the operating to increase the productivity. The motivation is reasonable, but shadow AI poses unique cybersecurity and commercial risks, just like any approved large language model (LLM) AI project.

### 3\. Research Methodology

Shadow AI often starts with well-meaning employees who just want to solve problems and work better with the help of AI technology. They're not trying to break the rules, just find solutions. The potential benefits and risks of Shadow AI usage in organisations and their data privacy along with the data classification for secure GenAI use were discussed with an extensive review of literature with the articles from 1995 to 2024 authors and report from the different internet sources and suggestions given. To find out the mismatch between IT and business by analysing by existing studies to identify key variables and relationships to conceptual model by considering the practical challenges and opportunities faced by organisations.

#### 3.1 Functionality Threats

Functionality threats were defined by the European Union Agency for Cybersecurity (ENISA). Functionality threats are created when an AI tool fails to perform as expected, leading to potential risks and consequences. Functional risk is modelling drift. It occurs when the AI model deviates from the problem space it was trained to handle, rendering it unusable and potentially misleading. Model drift may result from out-of-date training data or modifications to the technical environment. Operational risks which jeopardise the business operations of the company. There are various types of operational risks. For instance, a Shadow AI tool would help the company poorly if it is not properly trained. Negative advice from GenAI can lead to higher opportunity costs, such as when the company fails to invest where it should or to wasted investments, such as when the company grows carelessly. Using an LLM, especially any unlicensed one that was trained and developed outside of the enterprise's data management policy framework, it can also put confidential client and business information at risk. When a chatbot receives a user's prompt, it can utilise the information as training data and share it with platform operators and other users. In the event of a cyberattack against the AI platform, the data might also end up in the hands of criminals. When a healthcare provider summarises a patient's appointment using a GenAI chatbot, they are breaching HIPAA regulations and disclosing private health information. Talking points for an internal presentation are created by an executive using AI, disclosing trade secrets. When a lawyer organises case notes with a chatbot, information covered by legal protection that ensures the confidentiality of communications between an attorney and their client is revealed. A business analyst uses a GenAI app to create a new report by uploading a sizeable, multifield spreadsheet that contains customer credit card numbers ([Shevlane & Christiano, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib20)). Priorities for AI safety and governance should be the evaluation of models for extreme risks. With an LLM access to this kind of private information could jeopardise the company's business plans and intellectual property, giving rivals the upper hand and undermining competitive advantages. When personally identifiable information is involved, there may be major data privacy and compliance violations, harm to customer confidence and reputational consequences. Legal risks causes if the shadow AI puts the business at risk of legal action or fines, legal risks will come after operational and functional risks. However, the information is false, and the business wastes a significant sum of money by acting incorrectly. Investors may file a lawsuit. Suits may also arise if the model uses copyright-protected data for self-training or if the shadow tool gives users incorrect advice resulting from model drift or tainted training data and breaking data privacy laws can have serious legal repercussions.

#### 3.2 Regulatory Fines and Criminal Penalties

This may become more concerning as the laws governing AI development continue to change. In some cases, Shadow AI users could waste money by not taking advantage of special prices for similar, approved technology. Consider the opportunity cost associated with covert projects that eventually fail because they violate company policies or best practices. The time and money spent on these projects could have been used for other endeavours ([Ward et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib26)). If businesses intend to keep up with the constant advancements in technology, they will need to reconsider their Shadow IT strategies and gain a better understanding of how to handle its usage ([Silic & Back, 2014](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib22)). Limitations is an effective precautionary measure, but it is not a cure-all for Shadow IT issues that present opportunities for the whole ecosystem of the business concern. Moreover, be prepared to pay transition costs when shadow projects are formally added to the portfolio and cease to be shadow projects. Retraining will be required for the staff and employees who used the Shadow tool, so they can understand the tool set in its updated context and with new limitations. The organisation will also have to pay migration costs in order to move other users to the authorised tool ([Turpin et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib25)). Shadow IT is a phenomenon that privacy and conformance addresses, and it appears that this field is just getting started (Silic, 2013). Additionally, a number of earlier studies have already addressed this ‘insider-threat’ and its mitigation ([Warkentin, 1995](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib27)). The issue is one of employee awareness, and the suggested remedy is to train staff members to adhere to information systems security policies.

##### 3.2.1 Research Question: However, the Fundamental Query Remains: Why Is There Such a Stark Mismatch Between IT and Business?

Researchers have identified a number of reasons for this misalignment, including a severe lack of business-IT communication and a decline in responsiveness ([Teo & Ang, 1999](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib24)). Shadow IT is typically interpreted as a type of user non-compliance because it has been determined to be primarily an information systems security issue ([Györy et al., 2012](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib102)). Shadow AI frequently appears in businesses as a result of employees deploying AI solutions on their own, slow IT processes or a lack of AI tools [Widder et al. (2023)](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib28) address departmental needs. It can also arise from the perception that getting approval for new technologies is difficult. Applications that are not in compliance with the organisation's IT governance, such as unapproved machine learning models, AI-driven analytics tools or any other AI software, are referred to as ‘Shadow AI’. Workers frequently use Shadow AI to get around official channels, overcome bureaucratic delays, meet unmet needs with more specialised solutions or innovate and streamline work processes.

Enhancing IT agility, creating transparent AI governance frameworks, offering safe spaces for testing, encouraging open communication between IT and other departments and training staff members on risks and appropriate procedures are all necessary to prevent Shadow AI. In addition to the possible advantages, Shadow AI – unauthorised or unreported use of AI tools within organisations – can bring new risks. It's possible that Shadow AI models lack the same security precautions as systems that are authorised. This increases the risk of confidential information leaks, whether intentional (data theft by the model operator) or unintentional (sharing secrets). Unauthorised access to sensitive company data may occur if the Shadow AI tool involves a human review of customer data (for training or improvement, for example). Organisations are subject to legal requirements regarding cybersecurity and data privacy. Using shadow AI models outside of authorised channels may result in breaking these rules. AI models can produce erroneous results or reinforce preexisting biases, particularly when they have little training data (Wiltshire & Ronkainen, 2021). As the author pointed out, ‘hallucinations’ are essentially false information that the AI presents as fact, and they can have dangerous repercussions. Teaching staff members about the possible risks associated with ‘shadow’ AI and promoting appropriate use of these technologies. Defining precise policies and procedures for organisational approval of AI use is also needed. Security measures like ensuring all AI tools, authorised or otherwise, meet data security standards and comply with regulations. Regularly monitoring and auditing AI usage to identify and address any unauthorised or risky practices. By taking proactive steps, organisations can harness the power of AI while minimising the potential pitfalls of Shadow AI.

#### 3.3 Data Classification for Secure GenAI Use

Data classification is essential for secure GenAI use. It acts as a foundation for data security and responsible information management, similar to its role in general data security practices. It helps in all these ways like risk-based access control done by classifying data based on sensitivity, organisations can determine the appropriate level of access for GenAI tools. Sensitive data might be restricted to secure, on-premise AI deployments or enterprise-grade applications specifically designed to comply with internal data security policies ([Bai et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib2); [Bakhtin et al., 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib3)). Mitigating Shadow AI risks by classifying data which helps to identify information that should not be used with unauthorised or undocumented AI tools (Shadow AI). This reduces the risk of data breaches, unauthorised access and non-compliance with regulations. Informed decision-making allows organisations to make informed decisions about GenAI use cases. For instance, publicly available information could be used with consumer-grade chatbots while sensitive data would require a more secure and controlled environment. This policy applies to all employees, contractors and anyone authorised to use AI tools and resources provided by the organisation. It includes GenAI models (text generation, code generation, etc.), AI-powered applications (chatbots, data analysis tools, etc.) and Internal AI development platforms. An acceptable use policy for AI can make it very apparent whether using AI inappropriately can harm the company and how to coordinate (Matsuo et al., 2022). AI use with other risk-minimising measures like data security measures. Decision-makers can then swiftly determine risk exposure and the appropriate course of action by comparing the tools' use against the policy in the event that shadow AI emerges ([Castelfranchi, 2002](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib5)) teaching and Practicing Responsible AI Use for employees. To minimise the risks associated with Shadow AI and to get the most out of this technology, must provide the employee with the information and abilities they need to use AI responsibly.

*The following are some successful methods for education and training:*

To accommodate various learning styles, use a variety of formats, such as interactive modules, online courses, video tutorials and even simulations with real-life example throughout the course, and provide instances from your industry's responsible and irresponsible use of AI and case studies of actual Shadow AI occurrences to identify possible dangers and repercussions. Steer clear of excessively ([Castelfranchi, 2000](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib4)) technical jargon and concentrate on imparting useful information (refer [Fig. 4.1](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#fig1)).

#### 3.4 AI Fundamentals

[Zhu (2023)](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib31) offers the fundamental ideas behind AI, including its various forms, its constraints and its possible biases. Data Security and Privacy: When utilising AI tools, train staff members on data security best practices, such as data classification and access controls. Moral Aspects to Take into Account: Employees should receive training on how to identify and handle ethical issues with AI use, such as accountability, transparency and fairness. Recognising Shadow AI provide workers with the tools they need to recognise unapproved or unrecorded AI tools and comprehend the dangers involved. To Make Use of Various Training Techniques like E-learning Modules: Provide readily available web-based courses for introductory understanding and regular review. Workshops and Seminars: To go deeper into particular AI applications or tackle new challenges, arrange interactive workshops or seminars. Arrange casual get-togethers where professionals can talk about AI-related subjects and field questions from workers, able to voice concerns and queries regarding the use of AI without fear of retaliation. An internal platform for exchanging knowledge to provide a forum where staff members can exchange AI-related resources, best practices and experiences is needed. Consistent feedback systems to get employee feedback to find out how well they understood the training and what needs to be improved. Ensure that the AI training programme has strong leadership support and highlight how important it is to the success of the company. Continuous support to include AI best practices in performance reviews and day-to-day work procedures ([Cui et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib10)). Acknowledge and commend staff members who use AI responsibly. If employees are unaware of or do not comprehend AI policies, then their contributions are unproductive ([Rice et al., 2020](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib18), pp. 1–35). In light of this, give careful consideration to training employees on the safe and secure use of GenAI, either as a stand-alone project or as an extension of regular cybersecurity awareness training ([Milmo, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib14)). The dangers of using AI should be discussed in training as well, with a focus on data security and legal obligations. Deep learning algorithms are used to develop large (trained on massive text datasets from the internet), languages (operated based on human language) and models (used to make predictions) ([Orton-Johnson & Prior, 2013](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib15)). Users engage with LLMs like ChatGPT (GPT) to perform tasks like customer service conversations and content creation. The multidimensional task showcases the abilities of LLMs in text and image generation, summarisation, comparison and analysis ([Matsuo et al., 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib12)). LLM biases are a reflection of societal biases that are embedded in human language and culture. The development, instruction and use of AI systems all contribute to the perpetuation of these biases. The overrepresentation of socioeconomically advantaged groups in interactions with AI tools, such as internet users and English native speakers, combined with a lack of diversity among developers results in representational biases; preexisting stereotypes and societal discriminations are present in the datasets presented as historical biases (Collett & Dillon, 2019; [Cui et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib10)). According to [Ntoutsi et al. (2020)](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib105), these biases not only continue but also intensify discriminatory behaviours that are difficult to measure or quantify in daily life. Layers of interpretation entwined with data represent structural disparities associated with the intersectionality of gender, race, age and class, among other factors ([Joyce et al., 2021](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib103); [Singh, 2020](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib23), pp. 51–59). Compliance directly addresses each of the risk functions (Aziz & Dowling, 2019) of credit, market, and operational risk, it is perhaps most closely associated with enterprise risk management. The use of labour in conjunction with exposure to upsetting material without sufficient assistance serves as an example of how LLMs function as technology about relationships and systems, manifesting as real, covert exploitation of human endeavours ([Yin, 2009](#978-1-83608-298-920251004.xhtml#bib29)). Despite the recent attention given to generative text and image tools, businesses have been sluggish in establishing regulations for AI. Nearly half of senior leaders worry that large language AI models, like those used for writing, could create false information within the company. AI generating flawed legal documents and similar mistakes could happen with internal reports or client emails. Using AI to write code can be helpful, but it also carries risks. If an IT team relies on AI-generated code, it might contain hidden flaws that hackers could exploit. Additionally, some free AI tools record the prompts users provide, which could expose sensitive company data if it's included in a prompt. As governments establish AI regulations, companies need to be aware of them. Without someone monitoring these regulations, employees might unknowingly expose the company to legal trouble in the future.

### 4\. Conclusion

The realm of Shadow AI presents a complex and evolving landscape, demanding careful consideration of ethical, technological and organisational implications. As AI technologies continue to advance, it is imperative to strike a balance between innovation and responsible deployment. The risks associated with Shadow AI, such as unintended consequences, privacy breaches, and ethical dilemmas, cannot be ignored. Security and risk leaders must recognise the enduring nature of shadow AI. The proliferation of SaaS tools, on-premises development options and next-generation LLMs will likely fuel the growth of shadow AI projects. Understanding the motivations behind shadow AI adoption, such as the limitations of approved technologies, can help organisations develop effective mitigation strategies will be very important. *To navigate this landscape successfully, organisations should:*

*Embrace Transparency and Accountability:* Promote open communication and transparency within the organisation to foster trust and reduce the need for shadow AI.

*Foster a Culture of Ethical AI Development:* Establish clear ethical guidelines and provide training to employees to ensure responsible AI practices.

*Implement Robust Governance and Oversight:* Implement effective governance frameworks to monitor and manage AI development, including shadow AI initiatives.

*Invest in AI Education and Awareness:* Educate employees about the potential risks and benefits of AI to foster informed decision-making.

*Continuously Monitor and Assess Risks:* Regularly assess the risks associated with shadow AI and adjust mitigation strategies accordingly.

*Encourage Ethical Collaboration:* Collaborate with external stakeholders, such as researchers, policymakers and civil society organisations, to address the broader societal implications of AI.

By proactively addressing these issues, organisations can harness the potential benefits of AI while minimising the risks associated with shadow AI. The future of AI depends on our ability to navigate this complex terrain with ethical responsibility and foresight.
