## Chapter 5

## Influence of Ethical AI Practices on Gig Workers' Trust and Psychological Well-being

K. Shanthi and Suganya V.

Faculty of Management, SRM Institute of Science and Technology, Chennai, Tamil Nadu, India

### Abstract

This study investigates the ethical ramifications of the gig economy by analyzing the complex relationship between artificial intelligence (AI)-driven optimization, timely order completion, and gig workers' well-being, drawing on the utilitarian theory of ethics. The study's goal is to assess the ethical AI practices that affect gig workers' trust in AI and their psychological well-being. Qualitative research using an open-ended questionnaire and deductive thematic analysis guided by the proposed framework is used to examine the ethical use of AI in gig work. Overall, by addressing ethical considerations, AI-powered food delivery services can positively impact the psychological well-being of gig workers. Food delivery online services may provide advantages like convenience and accessibility, but they also give rise to significant ethical concerns that necessitate meticulous attention via transparent design, responsible execution, and regulatory supervision. The findings obtained from this study make a valuable contribution to formulating guidelines and policies to promote an ethically powered business. The research covers often-overlooked topics such as data privacy, algorithmic fairness, employment practices, and environmental sustainability. It also stresses the importance of proactive measures and stakeholder collaboration to ensure these services are ethical and benefit society.

*Keywords*: Gig workers; ethical AI practices; AI-driven optimization; utilitarian theory of ethics; trust in AI; deductive thematic analysis

### 1\. Introduction

Artificial intelligence (AI) is a technology that mimics human intelligence, revolutionizing human–technology interactions. It's central to the fourth industrial revolution and can significantly alter workforce structure, organization design, decision-making processes, and knowledge management. Trust in AI is crucial, especially within organizations. Scientists explore AI in various forms and levels ([Glikson & Woolley, 2020](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib14)). The gig economy, which is defined by temporary contracts and independent work, is being revolutionized by platform economies, which are powered by technologies such as blockchain, smart contracts, and AI. To succeed in this changing environment, individuals need to have a high level of digital literacy and the capacity to work effectively with virtual teams that are located in different regions ([Malik et al., 2021](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib23)). Gig economy platforms create international marketplaces for gig workers to showcase and monetize their skills. These digital workplaces integrate ICTs into a global information infrastructure, including Facebook groups, public forums, WhatsApp, PayPal, Amazon Pay, and navigation apps. Mobile internet and digital devices are essential for participation, with algorithms and machine learning tools optimizing gig work allocation ([Dedema & Rosenbaum, 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib11)). AI is being used in the gig economy to enhance productivity, streamline processes, and improve user experience. It aids in task allocation, pricing, route optimization, customer service, fraud detection, performance monitoring, maintenance prediction, and scheduling. AI-driven chatbots manage customer inquiries, authenticate identities, and provide personalized recommendations. However, challenges like algorithmic bias, transparency, and data privacy need to be addressed for ethical AI use in the gig economy ([Lippert et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib21)). The US Department of Commerce has proposed a new classification for gig economy businesses, “digital matching services,” which enable direct transactions, user feedback rating systems, hour determination and tool supply. These platforms facilitate connections between individuals and service providers ([Tran & Sokas, 2017](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib31)). In the following section, a review of the literature on gig work is presented. Following that is the methodology, which is a qualitative deductive thematic analysis. Next comes the conceptual model, then the discussion section, and finally, the conclusion and theoretical implications of the study are presented.

### 2\. Review of Literature

Gig work durations vary from minutes to years, and the technology role and work relationships are subject to change. Certain scholars limit gig work to microtasks that are conducted through digital platforms ([Caza et al., 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib7)); in our study, we have taken food delivery gig workers who work as delivery partners for Swiggy and Zomato, which offer autonomy through flexible shift timings and time off. However, algorithmic control can lead to excessive work, lack of sleep, and fatigue. Platform-based rating and ranking systems improve efficiency in labor process monitoring and supervision. Online labor platforms facilitate the connection between clients and a large and unregulated global pool of workers ([Wood et al., 2019](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib32)). The literature on the remote gig economy lacks clarity on job quality outcomes. ICTs influence organizational structures, with bargaining power affecting work arrangements and worker power. Social and environmental factors, such as health, education, gender norms, and interpersonal relationships, also influence job quality. The gig economy uses algorithmic management (AM), positioning customers as agents to ensure satisfaction and compliance ([Wood et al., 2019](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib32)). The online food delivery industry has witnessed a significant increase in its popularity. However, it is confronted with various challenges including substandard working conditions, absence of proper regulation, and job instability, all of which have adverse effects on the well-being and safety of gig workers. The viability of the online food delivery model in terms of sustainability is uncertain, as it may not be in line with the UN's Sustainable Development Goals, which prioritize equitable employment and positive health outcomes. The private sector should adopt a more comprehensive perspective on sustainability, taking into account improved working conditions and health outcomes for employees ([Quy Nguyen-Phuoc et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib25)). There is a lack of research on the ethical consequences of using AI in managing gig work, and there is a need for a conceptual model to analyze how these factors impact the well-being of gig workers. To address this gap in the literature, our research question for this study is as follows:

*How do ethical AI practices affect the trust that gig workers have in AI and impact their psychological well-being?*

A comprehensive framework for the ethical implementation of AI in the gig economy includes a variety of components, such as ecological resilience, ethical considerations, data privacy, algorithmic fairness, and employment practices. In the context of ethical considerations of AI implementation in food delivery gig work, it is imperative to adopt a comprehensive and multi-level perspective on organizational and community resilience when considering platform ecosystems (Floetgen et al., 2021). Consequently, we assessed all stakeholders and implemented a holistic approach. It encompasses principles such as accountability, transparency, fairness, and the effective resolution of complaints. To protect data privacy, it is essential to obtain explicit consent, implement stringent security protocols, and restrict the scope of data collection. To achieve algorithmic fairness, it is necessary to implement strategies that are designed to reduce bias, as well as to adhere to principles of equity and inclusivity. Employment practices encompass a variety of elements, such as job stability, fair remuneration, employee benefits, and career development initiatives ([Tan et al., 2021](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib30)) by career counseling in digital workspace. The concept of ecological resilience is collectively defined by the promotion of sustainable practices, the optimization of resource utilization, and the support of initiatives that are designed to offset carbon emissions and promote the use of renewable energy. Gig platforms have the potential to enhance the psychological well-being of their employees by emphasizing these factors, which in turn establish a conducive and equitable environment. The psychological well-being of gig workers is significantly influenced by the ethical implementation of AI and the establishment of trust in AI. Gig platforms have the potential to enhance the overall welfare of their workforce by establishing a conducive and equitable environment through the implementation of robust data privacy measures, equitable algorithms, optimal employment practices, and ecological resilience.

### 3\. Research Methodology

Qualitative research is done by using a multistakeholder approach ([Smets et al., 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib27)) to gather responses from researchers, the general public, customers, and gig workers. An open-ended questionnaire was distributed to the 32 participants through purposive sampling. The open-ended format of surveys allows respondents to select the topics that are significant to them ([Geer, 1991](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib13)). Participants were not asked questions about their identity or any other demographic information to maintain confidentiality, and they were assured that the data collected will be used only for research purposes. The constructs and conceptual framework proposed by the authors were the foundation for the questions, which were formulated using existing literature. Deductive thematic analysis ([Blum et al., 2020](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib5)) was employed to analyze the responses of the participants following the framework. The constructs in the proposed framework of the study are matched with the responses from the participants. Data collection was stopped once the data reached saturation ([Boddy, 2016](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib6)). The responses that are consistent with the themes are provided in quotes concerning each construct of the framework. The responses and the literature are used to propose seven arguments in this study.

### 4\. Conceptual Model for Ethical Application of AI in the Gig Economy

By addressing factors such as data privacy, algorithmic fairness, best employment practices, and ecological resilience, the conceptual framework (refer to [Fig. 5.1](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#fig1)) has been developed by authors to enhance the psychological well-being of gig workers.

![image](images/f05-01.jpg)

Fig. 5.1. Conceptual Framework. *Source:* Authors Conceptualisations.

### 5\. Discussion

In this section, we have presented seven propositions that are based on the existing body of literature and are further substantiated by responses that were received from a variety of stakeholders. The responses provided by the participants, which include various stakeholders in the food delivery industry, including gig workers, customers, the general public, and researchers, are presented in quotation marks and categorized for each construct.

#### 5.1 Data Privacy in Food Delivery Services

The digital nature of gig work platforms often leads to the neglect of privacy in the gig economy. Personal devices used by workers for monitoring purposes can create ambiguity between personal and work domains, thereby heightening the likelihood of inappropriate monitoring and potential violation of personal boundaries ([Sannon et al., 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib26)). A Zomato delivery partner mentioned that she is not familiar with the concepts of data privacy but expressed her fear of using the technology. A respondent, who has never used this food delivery service but is well-versed in the concept and a keen observer, expressed a general public opinion: “Gig economy companies should implement robust data security measures to protect users' and workers' sensitive information. They should also ensure that users receive clear privacy policies.” Workers in the gig economy face privacy risks that originate from both platform companies and customers. Platforms engage in comprehensive surveillance and store data on workers, enabling AM. Employees frequently face challenges in obtaining their data or resisting performance monitoring, which leads to demands for data protection rights and oversight of companies' surveillance practices. Additionally, platforms facilitate the monitoring of workers by customers, which leads to information imbalances. A customer of an online food delivery service explained his idea about data privacy, stating:

Most of the Gig Economy companies have effectively addressed the security concerns impacting various stakeholders. In the case of gig platforms like Swiggy and Zomato, one issue I can recall was contact info that used to be visible to the restaurant/delivery partner/customer. The gig platform resolves this issue by masking and routing the caller number. Furthermore, we should use certain data about customers, delivery partners and restaurants for effective targeted marketing, thereby promoting effective working practices. Putting a hold on all data under the guise of “data security” results in a “lose all” situation for all the stakeholders involved in the process, making the experience less smooth. I am all for giving up privacy and security for convenience, as the mere act of being online exposes us to a privacy threat.

In the gig economy, customers are in a managerial position, and their ratings have the potential to impact the ability of workers to secure employment. Advanced technologies, including geofencing, facial recognition, and telematics, can be employed to integrate customer surveillance with platform surveillance ([Sannon et al., 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib26)). A researcher shared his perspective on data privacy in the gig economy:

Gig economy companies should implement robust data encryption, strict access controls, and regular security audits to protect users' and workers' data. They should also adopt transparent data privacy policies and provide clear communication about data usage and protection measures. Furthermore, the protection of personal information necessitates multi-factor authentication and consistent updates to security protocols.

In place of customers engaging in surveillance with the implementation of data privacy measures, workers are likely to exhibit increased trust in AI. Hence, it is proposed as follows:

*P1. The adoption of strong data privacy measures by gig platforms will have a beneficial impact on gig workers' trust in AI.*

#### 5.2 Algorithmic Fairness in Food Delivery Service

The concept of “AM” implemented through platform-based rating and reputation systems has been identified as a highly influential method of digital control in the gig economy ([Wood et al., 2019](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib32)). AM is an emerging and insufficiently researched management strategy that integrates algorithmic control and algorithmic matching in AI solutions. Algorithmic control ensures that worker behaviors are in line with the objectives of the organization while algorithmic matching facilitates the coordination of interactions between demand and supply. Organizations utilize self-learning algorithmic mechanisms to efficiently accomplish their goals. The utilization of ICT in algorithmic systems is characterized by four fundamental attributes: Algorithmic comprehensiveness, algorithmic instantaneity, interactivity, and opacity. Algorithmic control mechanisms oversee the actions and whereabouts of workers and evaluate or incentivize their productivity. Tasks or customers are allocated to workers based on these results. Gig workers should possess a sense of perceived autonomy over their work, even in the face of algorithmic control, to foster trust in AI.

*P2. Ensuring algorithmic fairness in gig platforms will result in increased levels of trust in AI among gig workers.*

A delivery partner working in Zomato shared her experience with algorithmic control: “Sometimes there won't be any orders for long hours and the order-pay will be reduced drastically if there are high incentives.” A Customer shared his perspective:

Algorithms are mirrors of users' actions – just like your YouTube Algorithm will populate your feed with content that suits your taste, algorithms are tailored for individuals. This should not be confused as a lack of ‘fairness’ as these dynamics eventually are designed to cater to the user.

However, a significant number of food delivery gig workers have limited educational backgrounds, which hinders their comprehension and leads them to attribute their problems solely to the algorithm. These workers must receive proper training in algorithm fairness to address their issues. A researcher shared her doubt about bias in algorithm management in gig work:

Algorithms used by gig economy companies can exhibit bias, particularly if they are trained on biased datasets. For instance, they might inadvertently favor certain demographics over others, leading to unequal distribution of job opportunities or pay disparities. Bias can arise from factors like geographic location, user ratings, or even the type of tasks assigned, potentially reinforcing existing social inequalities.

Perceived control is the belief held by an individual that they can influence their internal states, behaviors, and external environment ([Pagnini et al., 2016](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib24)). According to [Infurna et al. (2011)](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib16), having a sense of control can reduce the chances of experiencing negative health effects. People who believe they have more control are more likely to take care of their health by engaging in preventive measures and physical activity. Perceived control is a psychological construct that reflects an individual's conviction in their capacity to exert influence and regulate their own life. It includes elements like possessing an internal locus of control (believing that one's actions determine outcomes), self-efficacy (confidence in one's abilities), mastery (feeling competent and skilled), and personal autonomy (having the freedom to make choices and decisions). A robust sense of control implies a firm conviction that an individual possesses the capacity to exert influence over the result of a specific circumstance. These individuals actively utilize coping strategies to effectively manage stressful situations, thereby protecting themselves from stress and increasing their chances of achieving positive outcomes ([Jung et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib17)). Enhancing employee control will have a beneficial effect on their perceived control, which in turn will positively influence outcomes such as job satisfaction and reduce physical and emotional distress as well as role stress ([Spector, 1996](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib28)). If individuals wish to go on a vacation, they can readily notify others and remain at their current location. There is no issue whatsoever. They are capable of functioning autonomously without any human supervisor to interrupt their work. Perceived control in gig work refers to an individual's understanding and interpretation of the information that is controlled by the labor platform. The individual's commitment, actions, and work recognition can be influenced by their personal value judgments, which are in turn impacted by this interpretation ([Lang et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib19)). AM is crucial for overseeing diverse workers, such as UPS delivery personnel, hotel housekeepers, retail staff, journalists, and physicians. The surge in remote work has rendered white-collar and managerial employees vulnerable to automated surveillance. Uber, as an illustration, employs a workforce of 26,900 individuals and oversees a network of 3.9 million drivers across the globe. AM in non-gig work settings is met with varying levels of approval but is generally acknowledged for its ability to enhance collaboration and optimize work procedures. Factors such as lack of transparency in algorithms and management, continuous monitoring of behavior, and lack of support can result in feelings of frustration, exhaustion, and a high rate of turnover among gig workers ([Zhang et al., 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib34)). These challenges can be mitigated through the implementation of effective algorithm management and the open sharing of knowledge regarding its implementation with the gig workers, who constitute the primary stakeholders.

#### 5.3 Employment Practices in Food Delivery Service

The employment practices in the food delivery service industry diverge significantly from traditional job roles. Gig workers experience autonomy, flexibility, and financial incentives. However, the autonomy and personal choices granted to gig workers give rise to an intricate work identity as they frequently engage in diverse jobs that involve hybrid roles. The market's response to these choices has implications for the worker's ego, identity, and finances. Gig workers face the challenge of balancing different work identities and dealing with the absence of a distinct work identity. This can be particularly difficult as gig workers often have to manage multiple jobs simultaneously ([Ashford et al., 2018](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib4)). The impact of gig work on worker autonomy and adaptability is not inherent, but rather a political construct shaped by socio-economic circumstances and technological platforms. While certain employees appreciate these advantages, others encounter severe working conditions as a result of their positionalities ([Anwar & Graham, 2021](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib2)). Work-related stress factors, such as conflicting roles, uncertainty, and excessive workload, can greatly contribute to the occurrence of work–family conflict (WFC). These stressors have the potential to extend into an individual's family life, creating challenges in separating oneself from work-related worries. Research has demonstrated that elevated levels of role overload, conflict, and ambiguity are linked to a rise in WFC. When employees are burdened by work-related responsibilities, they may require assistance in fully engaging with their family members, participating in family activities, or fulfilling their caregiving duties. Role conflict in the workplace can greatly affect employees' WFCs. The correlation between WFC and employees' psychological distress has been thoroughly examined in diverse industry sectors. Extended exposure to WFC can result in burnout, emotional fatigue, cognitive fatigue, depersonalization, and diminished personal achievement. Experiencing chronic stress due to the demands of juggling work and family obligations can result in feelings of being overwhelmed, anxious, and tense, which can contribute to psychological distress ([Abdou et al., 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib1)). Gig workers firmly believe that they possess a high degree of work flexibility. Work flexibility encompasses both temporal flexibility and the extent to which employees have the freedom to select their work location and methods. These choices can significantly influence various work and life-related factors, such as work-family balance and emotional exhaustion ([Ma, 2018](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib22)). Therefore, the presence of multiple role identities and excessive work demands can give rise to WFC, ultimately resulting in psychological distress. However, it is worth noting that work flexibility can provide some degree of assistance in this regard. The implementation of additional employment strategies aimed at enhancing individuals' well-being may foster trust in AI.

*P3. Implementing optimal employment strategies by gig platforms will support gig workers' trust in AI.*

However, gig workers are currently dissatisfied with their employment benefits, as evidenced by their response: “They offer medical insurance; however, there is a lack of clarity regarding how to utilize or claim it.” Additionally, they offer raincoats at a significantly reduced cost during periods of precipitation. Also, she stated, “Women are granted two paid rest days per month. However, there are specific working conditions that must be met to access them.” One of the respondents who is a customer thought:

Gig workers' working conditions don't meet the standards for decent work (DW). People who work as independent contractors don't have to work for companies that support their working conditions. Several trade unions for gig workers have brought up the issue of fair working conditions. However, I don't think this will help gig workers. Companies that focus on the gig economy are still pretty new, so I think we should deal with it as a matter of CSR and Decent Work (DW), rather than using a ‘stick’ to punish these new economic sources.

One of the respondents, who is a researcher, expressed his opinion that

gig workers frequently encounter difficult labor conditions, such as unpredictable income, inadequate job security, and inadequate labor protections. Gig workers are frequently required to work extended hours without the benefits and protections that traditional employees receive, which can result in financial instability and stress. Gig workers should be entitled to benefits such as paid time off and health insurance. Given the precarious nature of gig work, these benefits are essential for their well-being and can serve as a safety net that promotes their financial stability and health. The long-term financial planning and overall quality of life of gig workers can be negatively impacted by the low job security and stability they typically experience. Gig work's on-demand nature frequently leads to inconsistent employment opportunities, which complicates the ability of workers to establish a consistent income.

However, perceived job flexibility has a positive effect on personal, family, and business outcomes. An optimal workweek enables employees to effectively manage their work–life balance, resulting in advantages for both their well-being and their family dynamics. Furthermore, employees who can adjust their work schedule and location can work for extended periods without facing difficulties, highlighting the significance of flexibility in attaining a balance between work and family responsibilities ([Hill et al., 2001](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib15)). Low-skilled workers are being impacted by the changing landscape of employment, as they face challenges in attaining a sufficient income and job stability. The enhanced power imbalance resulting from the increased flexibility in the gig economy heightens the vulnerability to exploitation. The economically disadvantaged individuals, who have a preference for full-time employment but are compelled to work multiple part-time jobs, are becoming progressively more susceptible to vulnerability. The gig economy further exacerbates discrimination and economic inequality as workers frequently resort to precarious work arrangements due to limited alternatives ([Spreitzer et al., 2017](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib29)). The labor market is perpetually evolving, and the role of employees is pivotal. Work flexibility provides a harmonious integration of professional and personal lives, resulting in job contentment and optimal performance. The rise of digital technology and unconventional job positions has led to an increase in non-standardized forms of employment, which may affect employee job satisfaction levels ([Davidescu et al., 2020](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib9)). This situation should be regarded as an emergency by policymakers as it will subsequently impact their well-being and job performance.

#### 5.4 Ecological Resilience in Food Delivery Services

Traditional business models prioritize shareholder value while sustainable management focuses on environmental and social impacts. Sustainable capitalism generates long-term value by recognizing opportunities, mitigating risks, building trust, and ensuring fair work conditions. Linear economy models are being replaced by circular or gig economy models, adopting Industry 4.0 and applying 10R principles. Sustainable management aims for greater functionality with fewer resources, opening new markets, and enforcing social good regulations. AI can enhance ecological resilience by optimizing resource use, reducing waste, and promoting sustainable practices throughout the product lifecycle. It can identify inefficiencies, improve recycling processes, design sustainable products, and facilitate the transition from linear to circular economy models. AI can also monitor environmental conditions and make informed decisions to protect and enhance ecological resilience ([Lavinia, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib20)).

*P4. Gig platforms that exhibit ecological resilience by implementing sustainable practices will have a beneficial impact on gig workers' trust in AI.*

However, there are very divergent viewpoints among the general public regarding the environmental sustainability of gig work. “Innovative solutions that promote sustainability without compromising the core value proposition of gig work should be pursued by companies to balance economic considerations with their environmental impact.” Also, the respondent, who is a customer, concurred that he is unable to absorb additional costs:

I am fully committed to sustainable practices; however, I am unable to support the so-called ‘sustainable practices’ at the expense of longer wait times and increased expenses. I order online to save time that would otherwise be spent: (a) traveling to the restaurant, (b) waiting in a queue at the bill desk (observing a human seated there), and (c) waiting in a queue to have my order prepared and handed over until the hotel employee takes it and provides me with a meal. I typically place an order online when I am unable to allocate the necessary time to complete all of these tasks. It is futile to place an order online if it incurs a time-consuming process. I may be willing to forgo a few bucks in terms of increased expenses, provided that the sustainable practices that are purported to be implemented are legitimate and not a form of ‘greenwashing’. In other words, I am not comfortable with the idea of raising the price if the gig platform is unable to provide a detailed justification for sustainable practices that have a genuine impact.

However, another respondent, who is a researcher, was prepared to endorse environmentally friendly practices.

I would support gig economy platforms that integrate sustainable practices, regardless of the potential for increased expenses or extended waiting times. The long-term health of our planet is contingent upon sustainability, and it is a responsible decision to endorse eco-friendly practices. It may also serve as an incentive for other organizations to implement comparable policies, thereby fostering a more comprehensive environmental impact.

AI has the potential to greatly enhance the ecological resilience of the food delivery industry through the optimization of operations, waste reduction, and the promotion of sustainable practices. AI can examine traffic patterns, weather conditions, and delivery locations in real-time to enhance routes, diminish fuel usage and emissions, and guarantee quicker delivery durations. Additionally, it can forecast demand by analyzing past data, seasonal patterns, and current events, thereby minimizing excessive production and food wastage. AI can enhance inventory management by accurately forecasting the ideal quantity of ingredients required, thereby minimizing spoilage and minimizing waste. The system can suggest environmentally-friendly packaging choices by analyzing customer preferences and supply chain data. AI can enhance energy efficiency in operations by effectively controlling heating, cooling, and lighting systems. In addition, it can reduce periods of inactivity by assigning delivery tasks to drivers, providing environmentally friendly delivery choices, employing flexible pricing tactics, analyzing customer input to enhance services, and offering real-time monitoring and reporting on sustainability measures. The implementation process consists of several key steps, including data collection and integration, the development and training of AI models, the deployment of AI solutions, the monitoring and adjustment of performance, and the education of stakeholders.

#### 5.5 Trust in AI

Trust is an essential and fundamental concept when it comes to the utilization of technology, especially in the realm of AI. Vulnerability is the state of being willing to expose oneself to the actions of another party, regardless of one's ability to observe or regulate that party. Trust plays a crucial role in human–AI relationships because of the perceived risk associated with them, stemming from the intricate and unpredictable nature of AI behaviors. AI is widely regarded as gradually replacing various human occupations and fundamentally reshaping organizational frameworks. The susceptibility of low-skilled and low-cost employees to AI-driven replacement is uncertain in comparison to knowledge workers and top-level managers. Although AI is capable of performing certain human tasks, there is currently no occupation where every single task can be completely replaced by AI. Trust is a fluid concept that can be influenced by the actions of the individual or entity being trusted ([Glikson & Woolley, 2020](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib14)). The complexity of machine learning models makes trust a vital element in user-AI interactions. The growing interest in explainable AI (XAI) arises from the necessity to uphold user confidence. When AI is developed to gain user trust, it can be securely integrated into society ([Glikson & Woolley, 2020](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib14)). The technology acceptance model (TAM) is a conceptual framework that elucidates the behavior of technology adoption among diverse populations. The study initially proposed that perceived usefulness and ease of use are factors that determine one's intention to use a product or service in the future. Subsequent studies have integrated external variables, such as trust, which plays a vital role in the adoption of emerging technologies. Trust is formed through the combination of characteristics, intentions, and behaviors, which fosters mutual reliance and interconnection between individuals or groups. The TAM expands upon existing research on organizational trust and trust in technology as discussed by [Choung et al. (2022)](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib8). The objective of our research is to examine the role of trust in AI as a mediator and the ethical application of AI concerning the psychological well-being of gig workers in food delivery platforms. This is because trust in AI may lead to a more positive ethical implementation of AI for gig work.

*P5. Enhanced trust in AI will result in a more positive view of the ethical implementation of AI among gig workers.*

#### 5.6 Ethical Approaches in Food Delivery Services

The ethical frameworks for conducting business are based upon principles such as rights, justice, the common good, virtue, and utilitarianism. Rights are centered around the principle of treating individuals as autonomous beings whereas justice pertains to the equitable treatment of all individuals. The concept of the common good centers around the welfare of society, particularly for individuals who are less capable of taking care of themselves. Virtue prioritizes universal principles such as truthfulness and moral uprightness. Utilitarianism is a moral theory that prioritizes the maximization of positive outcomes and the minimization of negative consequences. Within corporate environments, it provides a convenient method for assessing ethical choices, but it may diminish the importance of other stakeholders' concerns and overlook intangible expenses ([Arogyaswamy, 2020](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib3)). Digital technologies, such as AI, are frequently advertised as being difficult to understand because of their ability to make decisions independently. Detractors contend that holding individuals accountable for the outcomes is no longer feasible, while automated decision-making yields fairer and just results. Nevertheless, the driving force behind these technologies is not primarily focused on design but rather on the influence of corporations. Researchers have devised techniques to evaluate and record ethical considerations, indicating that incorporating AI is a deliberate decision ([D'Cruz et al., 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib10)).

*P6. The ethical implementation of AI in gig platforms will positively influence the psychological well-being of gig workers.*

#### 5.7 Psychological Well-being of Gig Workers

Well-being in the workplace has expanded to management science and organizational behavior, focusing on employee well-being. It includes psychological and physical conditions like job satisfaction, burnout, and emotional exhaustion. However, some argue it doesn't consider the emotional aspect of work. Maintaining workplace well-being is crucial for personal and professional growth despite challenges like the gig economy and mobile internet usage. According to [Wu and Zhou (2023)](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib33), improving well-being can be achieved by acknowledging the worth of each individual and increasing job autonomy. However, progress in this regard can be impeded by factors such as economic insecurity, uncertainty about the future, and a lack of stable interpersonal support.

Gig workers consistently face distinct challenges in comparison to employees of traditional organizations. Gig workers encounter six challenges: viability, organizational, identity, relational, emotional, and career-path uncertainty ([Caza et al., 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib7)). Research in human resources management emphasizes the significance of security in the well-being of gig workers although there is a lack of consistency in the effects. Advocates contend that greater autonomy in gig work can result in enhanced well-being as conventional employment provides stability but also entails substantial compromises in terms of flexibility and remuneration. Gig workers, such as Uber drivers, value the ability to have a flexible work schedule and the ability to control fluctuations in their income. Reducing barriers to entry and implementing lower tax rates for gig-work platforms can have a positive impact on overall well-being, as suggested by [Felix et al. (2023)](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib12). An individual's quality of life, including work performance and productivity, is greatly influenced by their psychological well-being. It is shaped by emotional experiences, cognitive function, and job evaluation. Psychological well-being is associated with positive emotions, life and work satisfaction, and the absence of negative feelings. Satisfaction and happiness contribute to the enhancement of psychological well-being, which is indicative of an individual's interaction with social structures and conditions ([Kim et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251005.xhtml#bib18)). The psychological well-being of gig workers is the main objective of our research study. Workers' mental health may be significantly affected by the introduction of AI as they may experience fear and a lack of knowledge when using smartphones and this technology. Consequently, we aim to incorporate all ethical best practices for the use of AI in gig work.

*P7. Trust in AI and ethical AI use, data privacy, algorithmic fairness, best employment practices, and ecological resilience will improve gig workers' psychological well-being.*

Every single stakeholder involved in this food delivery service ought to be aware of the significance of trusting AI and the role that it plays in the psychological well-being of online food delivery gig workers.

### 6\. Conclusion

The ethical use of AI in the gig economy is complex. The task includes data privacy, algorithmic fairness, optimal employment practices, and ecological resilience. Data privacy helps gig workers feel safe and respected, encouraging them to use AI-powered platforms. No matter their background or demographics, algorithmic fairness eliminates biases and treats all workers equally. Gig workers' rights and welfare depend on fair compensation, manageable work schedules, and resources and perks. Ecological resilience is essential to the gig economy. By promoting green practices with AI, gig platforms can contribute to societal goals. This approach builds trust and purpose in employees. AI's ethical use and trust connect predictors to outcome variables. Transparent, equitable, and accountable gig worker rights and welfare practices build trust in AI. This framework aims to improve gig workers' psychological well-being. Gig workers can improve job satisfaction, and overcome stress, by prioritizing ethical AI practices and building trust through transparency, fairness, and respect. This holistic approach improves individual and collective gig economy results.

### 7\. Theoretical Implications

Our study's conceptual framework explains how ethical AI practices affect gig workers' psychological well-being. Data privacy, algorithmic fairness, best employment practices, and ecological resilience are predictors. The framework emphasizes trust in AI and its ethical use as mediators, emphasizing transparent and responsible AI practices. Ecological resilience is also included in ethical AI discourse to consider AI-driven platforms' environmental impacts. Psychological well-being is a key outcome variable in the framework, shifting the focus from economic or operational metrics to AI's human-centered effects in the gig economy. The framework helps policymakers and industry leaders implement ethical AI strategies. Focusing on AI as a mediator advances trust theory in technology adoption. The framework allows empirical validation and study design of ethical AI practices' effects on gig workers. These contributions help us understand the complex interactions between AI and human factors in gig worker food delivery online and other low-skilled on-demand physical services.
