## Chapter 6

## Decoding the Ethical Concerns in AI-Based Decisions Using Big Data

Anvesha Jain^a, Shrishti Singh^b and Ruchi Jain^b

^aIIIT-Delhi, India

^bAmity University, India

### Abstract

The aim of this study is to examine the ethical conundrums that arise when artificial intelligence (AI) is used to drive decision-making processes in various industries, with a particular emphasis on the ethical implications of employing Big data for decision-making, and also to identify and analyze the ethical issues that arise in various industries at various steps of the algorithm development process due to the usage of AI and Big data in decision-making. Analysis at the data collection stage led to ethical grey areas in the development of machine learning algorithms using biased datasets. Multidisciplinary research and case studies from a range of industries, including healthcare, finance, marketing, and education, were employed in the study process. Some of the main subjects addressed were invasions of privacy, impartiality and inherent bias in decision-making algorithms, the openness and explainability of AI systems, accountability in automated decision-making and the ethical implications of data ownership and control and their effect on data subjects. This research adds to a better understanding of the ethical environment surrounding Big data and AI applications by combining insights from many businesses. It emphasizes how crucial it is to take proactive ethical considerations into account when designing, implementing and governing AI-driven technologies in order to guarantee their responsible, advantageous and most crucially, consensual integration across industries. The study concludes with the consequences and implications of entrusting AI with human lives directly and indirectly and the ultimate question regarding the future of algorithms which govern humans.

*Keywords*: Big data; automated decision-making; ethical concerns; AI-based technologies; biasedness

### 1\. Introduction

In this decade, machine learning algorithms have advanced at a breakneck speed, and organizations have been trained using vast amounts of available data. Artificial intelligence (AI) has taken over the world, and now the scenario has shifted towards outcomes and predictions that these algorithms are making using Big data. Big data is not only the use of large amounts of gathered data but rather the interactions between various datasets. It comprises technology to handle the data, analyzing data to make claims and the myth that Big data can provide insights with exceptional accuracy. With the increasing relevance and popularity of Big data, it is necessary to analyze whether it is a phenomenon that has the potential to aid rapid, efficient advancements or if the seemingly dystopian future it entails will outweigh its benefits. One of the most anticipated discussions is the role of ethics in Big data. A case where supposedly anonymous data from Facebook were released and de-anonymized, raised questions about the use of public data without consent. Big data can be used by corporations to make decisions that do not take into account the privacy of the individuals whose personal data is being exploited ([Herschel & Miori, 2017](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib22)). Organizations such as Institutional Review Boards are set up as a response, prompting researchers to think critically about the ethical implications of their studies on stakeholders across the board. Questions about informed consent and accountability of data usage are raised. With AI being the buzzword around, organizations are joining the bandwagon but what are the odds of using the data ethically? This study raises striking questions that are not necessarily considered when having discourse regarding Big data usage by organizations. Perhaps the most important concern for the general public is the question of privacy, consent for their data being used and safety in terms of data breaches. So, the three mandatory aspects of data privacy, consent for usage and biases in AI systems are been addressed in this study.

### 2\. Literature Review

#### 2.1 AI and Big Data

The technical field of AI studies how computers exhibit intelligence through perception, synthesis and inference of information, which is different from the intelligence exhibited by people and non-human animals ([Ram & Verma, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib41)). It entails using algorithms inspired by human intelligence to simulate human-like methods to problem-solving, culminating in the creation of biomimetic robots with lifelike features and actions ([Alkurd et al., 2020](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib3)). Talking about Big data, it is the term used to describe the enormous volume of data that is gathered, saved and examined in order to derive insightful information and make defensible decisions in a variety of industries ([Rawat & Yadav, 2021](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib42)). Big data's exponential expansion has transformed decision-making processes and allowed businesses to use a variety of data sources for operational and strategic planning ([Arena & Pau, 2020](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib4)). Studies reveal a notable surge in Big data publications, emphasizing the multidisciplinary nature of the field and the changing trends from technological challenges to methodological problems and the advancement of AI ([Tagay et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib49)). Big data and AI are entwined in today's technological innovations, with the potential to revolutionize a multitude of fields. Large datasets may be accurately and efficiently analyzed thanks to the combination of Big data analytics with AI approaches like machine learning and evolutionary algorithms ([Gomathi Meena & Dharani, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib19)). Innovation is sparked and decision-making processes are improved by this integration's ability to extract insightful knowledge and valuable insights from intricate data structures ([Gandomi et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib17)). In addition, the application of AI and Big data to product design methodology transforms conventional methods by fusing real-time data with consumer preferences and market demands to provide customized and intelligent designs ([Quan et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib40); [Rikap & Lundvall, 2021](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib43)). The convergence of AI and Big data not only boosts productivity but also tackles issues like security, governance and regulatory compliance in the dynamic field of intelligent systems and technologies ([Quan et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib40)).

#### 2.2 Ethical Considerations in AI

The Greek word ‘ethos’, which means ‘character’, is where the word ethics first appeared. Ethics is the branch of philosophy that studies how people behave in social situations, offering logical defences of moral conclusions, identifying what is morally acceptable or unacceptable and differentiating between what is morally just and what is morally unjust ([Pan, 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib37)). The study of reasonable arguments, ethically good or bad, and just or unjust, for the responsible development and application of AI technology is known as AI ethics ([Gao et al., 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib18)). The utilization of Big data by AI raises a number of complex ethical questions that cut across several fields ([Schultz & Seele, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib45); [Siau & Wang, 2020](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib46)). Concerns about data privacy, manipulation and misuse are brought up by Big data-driven AI applications, which put people and society at danger ([Agbese et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib1); [Christoforaki & Beyan, 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib11)). The convergence of AI and Big data in social science research brings up issues with risk management, subject protection, methodological biases and social harms ([Hermansyah et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib21); [Mensah, n.d.](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib33); [Zohny et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib52)). The ethical implications of AI in social systems, like social computing, also highlight the necessity of designing with society's interests, vulnerable populations and responsible data collecting and processing in mind ([Bleher & Braun, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib7); [Choung et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib10); [Díaz-Rodríguez et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib12)). These discussions highlight how crucial it is to take ethical issues into account when developing and implementing AI systems that use Big data in order to guarantee their benefits for both people and society at large.

### 3\. Research Methodology

The research methodology used for the purpose is case methodology to comprehend why and how companies extract Big data and use it in decision-making with the help of AI in an ethical way. The ethical questions regarding bias, transparency and accountability of the decision-making processes used to harvest data and make predictions using models were interrogated. It also delved to assess how this can operate in sync with rest of the organizational strategies. For the purpose, mixed methodology was used by considering personal interviews of the top managers, surveying the clients of the company and assessing the impact of AI for decision making and the ethical dilemmas surrounding it. Case studies from different industries like healthcare, finance, marketing and education were referred. A thorough analysis of steps taken by various organizations, industries and governments to address these ethical issues for public purview was conducted through blogs and research articles available. The issues like invasions of privacy, impartiality and inherent bias in decision-making algorithms, the openness and explainability of AI systems, accountability in automated decision-making, the ethical implications of data ownership etc. are analyzed. The information about companies taken as case in point is also synthesized to harness the pertinent ethical issues related to the topic of use of AI in decision-making.

### 4\. Theoretical Underpinning

#### 4.1 Information and Computer Ethics (ICE)

The farfetched concept of information and computing ethics (ICE) was appended by [Floridi (2006)](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib15) with the details of philosophy of information (PI) and correlative information ethics (IE). Digital landscape demands for interrogation of interactions between human and devices with innovative methods. The PI implied the comprehension of digital ecosystem, with ethical contexts, information required and the involved organization for deciphering changed civic interactions and behaviour, whereas the IE denoted the ethical perspective while creating, using or sharing information in the digital ecosystem. IE is crucial for ethical behaviour, data privacy and ethical usage of information in all the decision-making processes ([Estelle, 2019](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib13)).This framework reports the role of AI in defining ethical considerations within information societies, propagating consideration of global ethical issues rather than specific applications in the infosphere ([Bruneault & Laflamme, 2021](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib8)). This framework make basis for most of the studies pertaining the ethical concerns while using AI-based decision-making in a variety of context as discussed in the next section.

#### 4.2 AI for Decision-Making

The presence of biases in AI algorithms is a matter of serious concern because AI algorithms are increasingly being used worldwide for decision-making purposes. Even though AI may be efficient in aiding the process of decision-making, the transparency of the process is debatable. The AI's algorithm can be a black box, and it is not clear how it self-learns, hence lacking in transparency. It can be further proved by the fact that the most popular AI chatbots like ChatGPT and Gemini are closed-sourced and being used in various fields. One person alone can train them on a dataset without the intervention of anyone else. The understanding of process will lay foundation for further discussions. There are multiple stages involved in making policies using AI.

##### 4.2.1 Setting Priorities

In the first stage, it can help the decision-makers identify the most urgent tasks by analyzing large datasets and data collected through various means. It can detect patterns that can guide the decisions. For example, the Victorian government uses AI for advanced text analysis of anonymized data to form health-related policies. Similarly, AI is also being used in France to aid the health sector (Wirjo & Calizo, 2024).

##### 4.2.2 Making Policies for Decisions

In the second stage, AI can directly lead to decision-making. AI's ability to quickly assess large amounts of data and predict future outcomes can help in various spheres. For example, in the economic sphere, the AI framework ‘AI Economist’, created by Salesforce, has been shown to formulate economic policies by predicting the costs and benefits of the possible policies and properly recognizing the target audience. The United Nations Conference on Trade and Development (UNCTAD) has also created an AI to predict the implications of trade policies on global trade (Wirjo & Calizo, 2024).

##### 4.2.3 Streamlining Decisions

In the third stage, AI can improve the efficiency of the decision-making process. It can simulate, test and forecast the possible effects of policies. This will help in speeding up the decision-making process. For making informed decisions with the help of AI, it is crucial to understand the sources of Big data and how these data are extracted for analysis purpose as these are the responsible variables which induce the inherent biases and makes the foundation of ethical issues.

### 5\. Harvesting Big Data for Algorithms

Informed decisions are based on actual data that are harvested from a vast audience. Quantitative data from applications such as Instagram, Twitter and transaction services are collected in the form of likes, comments, tweets and other transactions. Qualitative data take the form of surveys, online forums, blogs etc. It is put through various methods of qualitative analysis to yield a quantifiable result. Appropriate data are then extracted and organized into the requisite structure. Now, a machine learning algorithm can be trained to process these data and recognize all possible patterns. These patterns once developed can be utilized to predict a ‘target attribute’. The validity and correctness of the algorithm in target prediction is thoroughly analyzed and tested before market release. Throughout this process, there arise multiple questions regarding the data subject's consent and privacy at every step. More often than not, there is no active information regarding consensual usage of personal information that an individual may wish to keep private. These vast amounts of data have a large market.

#### 5.1 Inherent Biases of Big Data

Big data enables to draw conclusions based on vast amounts of privately or publicly available data. It shifts how one learns and gains knowledge by instigating approaches such as data driven decision-making etc. Currently, various AI frameworks are using them as resource and apply them to specific decision-making problems ([Zhuang et al., 2017](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib51)). The question of inaccuracy and misinterpretation is one of the first concerns that the usage of Big data embodies. It is not true that large data samples are representative enough to be used to make important decisions that affect human lives. The sheer size of Big data can also lead analysts to confuse it with the statistical ideal of a ‘population’, when it is actually a biased sample ([McFarland & McFarland, 2015](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib32)). The fairness of Big data is the first concern in ethical decision-making.

#### 5.2 Big Data Is Not Simultaneous to Better Data as Methodological Issues Remain Relevant Even With Big Data

Using Twitter as an example, it is illustrated how Big data presents challenges related to sample representation and accountability. It also advocates for recognizing the value of ‘small data’. Small data is a strategy that has been remarkably successful, enabling various disciplines to advance in leaps and bounds ([Kitchin & Lauriault, 2015](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib25)). Why, then, must we turn towards Big data entirely? The next focus is on the importance of context while using Big data. To analyze connections between messages and accounts on social media platforms, using data from only publicly accessible accounts is a matter of investigation. In contrast, historical methods used for collecting data about people's relationships through instruments such as surveys, interviews, etc. emphasize the value of understanding personal networks.

#### 5.3 Limited Access to Big Data

It is crucial to understand that access regarding Big data creates a digital divide. Access to proprietary datasets is restricted, with large corporations being the sole authority dictating how their data can be accessed. The skill sets are required to work with Big data, restricted to those with a computational background. The use of Big data induces critical reflection on the implications for future research and technology built on it.

#### 5.4 Big Data–Based Decisions and AI Biases

Organizations utilizing the user's data to make decisions by powering machine learning/AI algorithms has become a significant concern in recent years. The AI can assist in and directly lead to decision-making in various spheres including, but not limited to, the economic, educational, healthcare, tax and social spheres. Within these spheres, it can create decisions for optimization, welfare, fraud detection, personalization, etc. It is common practices of various countries to harvest user data for various purposes, to make predictions and decisions which impact users' lives. The methods used for data collection vary from traditional census surveys to the use of advanced surveillance technologies such as monitoring online activities, tracking mobile phone locations and even using facial recognition as large datasets. The algorithms are employed in various domains, including healthcare, law enforcement and social services, However, using user data to power AI algorithms raises concerns about the potential for biases to be amplified and perpetuated. The algorithms rely on the data they are trained on, and if these data are biased or incomplete, the outcomes produced by the algorithms may also be biased. AI algorithms have been found to exhibit biases related to gender, race, ethnicity and criminal record, among other factors. These biases can have far-reaching implications for users, whom algorithmic decisions may unfairly impact. One example of racial and ethnic biases is AI algorithms used in law enforcement, which have come under scrutiny for disproportionately targeting minority communities. The use of predictive policing algorithms is considered in the case of facial recognition technology. In a study assessing such disparities, it was shown that the maximum error rate for lighter-skinned males is 0.8%; however, the error rate for dark-skinned females can go up to 34.7% ([Buolamwini & Gebru, 2018](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib9)). And the datasets are overwhelmingly composed of lighter-skinned subjects. This shows that facial recognition algorithms exhibit higher error rates when identifying individuals with darker skin tones, leading to disproportionate misidentifications and false positives among people of colour. This has significant implications for law enforcement and surveillance practices as misidentifications can result in wrongful arrests and infringements on civil liberties. Another significant example of racial and ethnic biases in AI algorithms pertains to immigration policies and border control. Governments employ AI-driven systems to analyze vast amounts of data to determine immigration eligibility. However, these systems have been found to exhibit biases against individuals from specific racial or ethnic backgrounds, leading to discriminatory treatment in visa approvals, border screenings and immigration enforcement. Minority communities, particularly people of colour, often face heightened scrutiny and profiling based on algorithms that prioritize specific demographic characteristics or perceived risk factors. This in-depth analysis indicates that datasets being used to train AI algorithms are already biased, and the results represent these biases in the form of ethnic, gender and racial disparities. Since data collection and initial test runs are the first step towards releasing AI-based policies, services or products, it can be assumed that the foundations of these algorithms (that will ultimately affect users) are already treacherous.

### 6\. AI in Governance: Ethical Concerns

While AI doesn't directly write legislation, it's increasingly used to analyze data and suggest strategies that shape policies with significant real-world consequences for citizens. It can automate tasks, leading to faster processing of applications, reduced bureaucracy and potentially improved services. The analysis of the policymaking process using AI raises some obvious questions: What are the policies/structures that employ AI algorithms in the global scenario, and how are these policies transforming and affecting citizen lives? Let's explore three current and upcoming policy areas where AI is playing a major role, examining the impacts and public reception. In the criminal justice system, AI algorithms are used for tasks like predicting recidivism rates to determine bail amounts and parole eligibility. Proponents argue for efficiency and reduced incarceration costs. However, critics point out inherent biases in the data used to train these algorithms. For instance, if past arrest data informs the model, it could perpetuate racial profiling, leading to harsher sentences for minorities. This has sparked outrage, with movements like ‘Black Lives Matter’ calling for fairer justice systems untainted by AI bias ([Lageson, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib26)).

AI-powered ‘smart cities’ initiatives use cameras and sensors to manage traffic flow, optimize resource allocation and even monitor public safety. While proponents hail improvements in efficiency and safety, concerns arise regarding privacy intrusion. Citizens might feel constantly watched, with their every move tracked and analyzed. Debates rage about the appropriate balance between security and individual freedoms ([Galič, 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib16)). Social welfare programs are increasingly incorporating AI for tasks like benefit allocation and fraud detection. While AI can streamline processes and identify fraudulent claims, it can also lead to impersonal and unfair treatment of citizens. Algorithms might mistakenly deny benefits to those who genuinely qualify, creating hardship and frustration. Additionally, some fear such systems could create a social credit system, where government services are tied to a citizen's ‘worthiness’ determined by AI (Ramond, 2024).

By enabling new methods of audience engagement, outcome measurement and value creation, Big data and AI are also revolutionizing the area of public relations (PR). It is being used for PR campaigns by developed economies such as the United States, Japan and the United Kingdom, which raises ethical and legal questions. The collection and analysis of large volumes of personal data for targeted campaigns raises ethical questions about consent and privacy ([James, 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib24)). The application of AI in healthcare and medical education is equally expanding, making ethical application concerns more crucial than ever. AI enhances medical procedures including imaging, prognosis, diagnosis support and therapy recommendations for human health. The ethical and societal concerns surrounding the use of AI in healthcare are similar to those surrounding the use of personal data, function automation, reliance on assistive medical devices and ‘telehealth’ ([Leimanis & Palkova, 2021](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib28)). AI algorithms are being used by American healthcare providers to help them make judgements about patient care, including which patients need special attention or access to medical privileges. Researchers at UC Berkeley find indications of racial prejudice in algorithms ([Obermeyer et al., 2019](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib36)). Big data analytics and AI have been employed by fintech companies to evaluate vast amounts of data from several sources and generate independent recommendations or assessments ([Li et al., 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib31)). By combining AI and Big data, fintech companies may offer more personalized financial services, improve operational efficiency and reduce costs ([Ashta & Herrmann, 2021](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib6)). But there are also privacy and ethical challenges with the combination of AI with Big data, including those related to ownership, control, justice, transparency, bias and discrimination ([Hermansyah et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib21)). Lack of transparency in AI-driven credit score or loan approval procedures can unfairly disadvantage some people, affecting their opportunities and financial stability ([Laksamana et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib27)).

The recruitment process has been completely transformed by the use of AI and Big data by enterprises. AI systems can provide a proficient automated hiring system that businesses may utilize to choose the most qualified applicants based on their qualifications. In video interviews, many companies are moving towards algorithms that score candidates based on several attributes ([Li et al., 2021](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib30)). However, several recent applications have ethical problems that violate equality laws; it is apparent that the hiring process is unjust and discriminatory. When it comes to hiring, unconscious racism, ageism and sexism are major factors. Acknowledging and minimizing these prejudices is essential ([Gonzalez et al., 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib20)). These are just a few examples of how AI is shaping policy. It has a greater impact on the citizens' lives. Algorithmic bias can lead to discrimination in areas like loan approvals, job applications, and even criminal justice. Citizens from marginalized communities often bear the brunt of these biases. Many AI-driven policies lack transparency. Citizens often don't understand how decisions are made, making it difficult to hold authorities accountable for unfair outcomes. AI-powered surveillance raises concerns about individual privacy and the potential for misuse by authorities.

### 7\. Findings: Harnessing Ethical Ecosystem for AI-Based Policies

AI's influence on policymaking is undeniable. While it offers potential benefits like improved efficiency, its impact on citizens' lives can be profound. Addressing concerns about bias, transparency and privacy is crucial. Open dialogue, public scrutiny and robust regulations are essential to ensure AI remains a tool for good, not a force for social division and control. By harnessing the power of AI responsibly, we can build a future where technology empowers citizens and fosters a more just and equitable society. For these reasons, certain measures must be enforced at various levels to ensure that AI is used responsibly and well within constitutional limits. It is necessary to investigate the structures that limit the usage of AI-based algorithms for policymaking purposes, their current domains and whether these bodies are successful in their goals of achieving safe and accountable AI usage. AI frameworks regulating policymaking are still in the initial stages of development in most governments and organizations who intend to deploy AI algorithms.

#### 7.1 Organizational Level

The large corporations such as IBM have appointed an ‘AI Ethics Council’ consisting of various IBM stakeholders to review and make decisions regarding various policies and products ([IBM, n.d.](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib23)). Although information regarding the AI Ethics Board has been made publicly available, there is scarce information regarding the process and capacity in which these board members are appointed the decision-making authority. On the other end of the spectrum, it is highly interesting to note that Microsoft has recently dissolved their ‘ethics and society team’ that ensured responsible decisions revolving around their AI services and tools ([Newton, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib35)), opting to instead retain an ‘Office of Responsible AI’. The report claims that the changes suggested by the ethics team to protect the interest of consumers were discarded by Microsoft. These statements lead us to believe that ethical boards in such corporations do not have a final authority on decisions regarding AI products. Consequently, these boards do not hold much importance when it comes to ensuring responsible and ethical implementation of AI; hence, it can be speculated that organizations have passive ethical boards as a scapegoat to disperse questions of accountability and responsibility in the development of AI products. It also provides a certain sense of relief and satisfaction to the consumer in simply knowing about the existence of such ethical boards, as the average customer would not perform a deep delve into the actual power that is possessed by these boards.

#### 7.2 National Level

Moving towards a bigger picture, a publicly available document, ‘Executive Order 13960’, designed for the regulation of AI products and services in the United States states that AI is encouraged within the limits of law, order and safety of the citizens. Its policies also address questions of responsibility that are to be upheld when government policies using AI algorithms are deployed ([Federal Office of the President, 2020](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib39)). However, these rules do not make explicit in what manner exactly are agencies required to present monitoring records, ensuring transparency in their process. The consequences for not complying with these policies are also not made explicit. Towards the end of the document, it is also stated clearly that this order does not apply to AI being used in the defence department, providing no further explanation for the same. Even if large governments like the United States are ensuring AI is getting deployed within certain limits, these regulations fail to ensure transparency to the citizens (instead of just the government) who are the subjects of the algorithms. AI systems that process the personal data of EU residents are impacted by the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR), which establishes a standard for data privacy in the EU. In particular, these regulations' emphasis on permission, data minimization and the right to explanation are pertinent to the transparency and accountability of AI ([Patel, 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib38)).

#### 7.3 Global Level

At an international level, the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD), which is a transnational body with more than 30 member countries, has set up OECD.AI. OECD.AI has been described as a forum to shape trustworthy AI (Aaronson, 2022). It has also set up AI principles that require the member countries to adhere to. Unfortunately, there is no way to ensure that it can encompass a large number of member nations ready to comply with these principles as the decision to join OECD is also influenced by multiple sociopolitical factors and international relations amongst the member nations. It can be said that there is no exhaustive set of rules applying to all countries on the map that protects citizens from the misuse of AI and holds bodies accountable while ensuring a transparent process. The United Nations is a large international organization which has yet to come up with a strategy for this problem, apart from principles ensuring limits on AI within its systems.

### 8\. Conclusion

Now attention can be focused on the direct implications and consequences of the unbridled usage of AI-based algorithms and what the future of AI in governance can entail through detailed examples and attractive features that AI has introduced in lives. The digital age is an algorithmic archipelago, a vast landscape where algorithms shape the experiences whether liked or not. Personalization is a way to provide individual users with the information and services that is relevant to them. While it is desirable, AI algorithms raise concerns about giving them so much control over our lives. Algorithms curate information based on past behaviour, creating what we call ‘filter bubbles’ that reinforce existing beliefs ([Samuels, 2012](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib44)). Many social media platforms, like Facebook and Instagram, promote engagement by recommending content which more often than not confirms the users' bias ([Leonidas et al., 2017](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib29)). This can lead to dichotomy in the society which is undesired. For instance, in the 2016 US elections, filter bubbles may have contributed to the spread of misinformation as a result influencing the voters ([West & Bergstrom, 2021](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib50)). They can also inherit and amplify social biases because of the data that they were trained on. For example, facial recognition software has been known to be more accurate for fair skin tones, potentially leading to discriminatory policing practices. Now whether that was because the data which the model was trained on had more fair skin toned people is a completely different thing, but in the end, this is one of the many challenges we would be facing. Nowadays even resume screening is done using these algorithms, and if we aren't careful enough the algorithm might just be filtering out candidates on the basis of something totally unrelated to the job. This raises concerns about equal opportunity and even potential lawsuits based on algorithmic bias. As the algorithms become more sophisticated, they may even start dictating the choices we make even if they weren't designed to do so. The so-called ‘Recommendation Systems’ can nudge us towards specific products and services, restricting our options without us knowing. As societies become increasingly dependent on algorithms, there's a growing need for transparency. Understanding how these algorithms work and the data they are trained on is crucial for their accountability. There are many ongoing efforts to develop regulations that promote transparency and fairness in algorithmic decision-making. For example, the European Union's GDPR grants individuals the right to access and control their personal data used in algorithms. Another one of the challenges which we dwell upon are, the spread of misinformation and ‘fake news’ in this digital age. Algorithms which are programmed/not programmed to promote engagement over truth can amplify the impact of such things thereby creating a false narrative. Additionally, the opacity of some algorithms such as ML models or DL models creates difficulties in tracking which content they recommend ([Esteves, 2022](#978-1-83608-298-920251006.xhtml#bib14), pp. 39–59). In conclusion, in this digital age the algorithmic archipelago presents both challenges and opportunities. While things like personalization and convenience are undeniable upsides, the potential downsides can't just be ignored. To have a sustainable growth of this landscape as well, we will have to be mindful of the power we give to these algorithms. Promoting transparency, developing legal frameworks to address the bias generated if any and promoting critical and ethical thinking are crucial to make sure that algorithms serve, rather than subjugate, our humanity. From a research perspective, it can be concluded that apprehensions surrounding Big data can take the form of accessibility, vulnerability, representativeness, etc. while deploying the decision-making capabilities of AI systems that are wholly based on decades of data collection using Big data.

### 9\. Future Dilemmas

It is to ponder who exactly can be held responsible for limitations and consequences of Big data usage – the large corporations that collect data or researchers that use it? And even if somehow accountability is specified, how exactly will the people or systems responsible undo the existing apparatus that employs Big data heavily? How can the general public with little knowledge of these systems be reassured of correct usage of their data? These are the questions that individuals and organizations with the necessary resources and knowledge (and hence power) are still unwilling to answer.

## Chapter 7

## From Ideation to Publication: Ethical Practices for Using Generative AI in Academic Research

Jaswinder Pal Singh, Neha Mishra and Babita Singla

Chitkara Business School, Chitkara University, Punjab, India

### Abstract

There are numerous ethical, social, and legal considerations associated with generative artificial intelligence (AI) in academic research, from ideation to publication; generative AI contributes an important role in academic research. The creation, rewriting, and enhancement of the creative AI document have changed academic writing. The AI tools help researchers to develop new data and ideas through contextual understanding and human-like responses. While AI has many advantages, its use in academic research presents ethical concerns, particularly about authorship and IP rights. Collaborating with AI systems can be challenging as they are prone to writing errors. Similarly, AI-generated content is ethically uncertain due to a lack of recognition of AI ownership and accountability in legal systems. This study encourages ethical practices through the transparency of AI in academic research. Understanding the concepts of AI is crucial for relying on and using AI results in academic research to save academic integrity. However, AI specifications are suitable to improve educational ethics and acceptance. Although AI can create tech jobs, it can also eliminate mundane jobs as well as can transform education and communication. The study also emphasizes AI-generated material accountability for harmful principles, practices, advantages, and disadvantages. The design, deployment, and monitoring of AI systems must be ethical to avoid bias and misrepresentation. The study concludes the economic and social impact of generative AI in academic research. It is vital to conduct further research in order to develop comprehensive ethical AI practices that can be implemented in the future.

*Keywords*: Generative AI; ethical practices; academic research; ideation; publication

### 1\. Introduction

The scientific community is significantly excited by the application of generative artificial intelligence (AI) in academic research. Researchers are increasingly utilizing AI-powered technologies to assist them at different phases of the academic research writing process ([Khan, 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib36)). Writers utilize generative AI approaches to create initial manuscripts, which also offer insightful recommendations for enhancing previously written content ([Sands et al., 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib66)). Generative AI technologies provide academics a novel means to produce ideas, broadening the realm of research writing and stimulating generative AI thought processes. The widespread adoption of generative AI practices has fundamentally changed the domain of academic writing. Generative AI is likely to improve academic research writing, but it also raises some important ethical questions. In academia, there is a debate over whether we should use AI and whether it should handle educational tasks at all partly because doing so can raise questions about the credibility of AI-generated content because of the current lack of oversight over its construction and use yet if we at the academy decide that some use of AI in a scholarly context is beneficial and ethical, how do we handle the authorship question? Investigations have begun to raise doubts about whether generative AI systems can be considered authors of scientific studies, not least because of their failure to accept responsibility for the aforementioned actions and (in this case very important) consequences of being an author. Even when it comes to considering what can be called the “authors” most rudimentary, their authenticity is attributed to the extent that even an AI-designed paper can be compared to actual scientific discourse. Our society has put a lot of scrutiny on the issue of authorship. Generative AI systems have no legal basis to claim ownership of the document produced. This is because the law only allows natural humans or recognized legal entities to do so. Cognitive research that demonstrates the application of AI is vital to its widespread acceptance as it is an important element of the methodology. According to [Bukar et al. (2024)](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib14), the modern AI system has the potential to produce language that appears to be logical and important, but is in fact delusional. The term used to describe the event is “artificial fraud” ([Sison et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib70)). Recognizing AI restrictions and threats helps authors to maintain the integrity of their work ([Monteith et al., 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib51)). The datasets used to train AI systems can reinforce and contaminate biases. This can spread misinformation that encourages unethical behavior and bad thoughts ([Krammer, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib40)). The authors' secret use of generative AI makes it difficult to detect and optimize bias in research participants.

### 2\. Generative Artificial Intelligence in Academic Research

In academic research, AI-based writing tools assist authors at every stage of the writing process as shown in the [Fig. 7.1](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#fig1) and ensure accuracy and excellence ([Raman, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib59)). Natural language processing facilitates the organization and enhancement of written works by generating and comprehending language that resembles human speech ([Suryanto et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib75)).

![image](images/f07-01.jpg)

Fig. 7.1. Ethical Considerations in Generative AI. *Source:* Authors' own conceptualization.

Editors and reviewers have the ability to utilize automated peer-review platforms, and this system efficiently assesses numerous submissions, resulting in time savings ([Rousi et al., 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib65)). AI-based technologies offer a significant advantage by effectively reducing the time required for academic writing as well as plagiarism detection technology helps to identifying copied text. However, by utilizing natural language processing techniques, authors may prioritize the content of their writing over its technical aspects ([Sathe et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib67)). Similarly, AI algorithms can enhance the efficiency of comprehensive literature research and decrease processing time for activities such as language translation and text summarization. Natural language processing systems can generate customized outlines for various types of written documents, such as emails, reports, research protocols, grant proposals, informed consents, insurance letters of medical necessity, and more ([Jayakumar et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib31)). In addition, it should be noted that language processing has the capability to offer precise recommendations, thereby enhancing the quality of a paper. Additionally, the AI tool may provide recommendations for an optimal study sample size and advice the most suitable statistical test to perform based on prior research ([Abbasian et al., 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib1)). Integrating AI into academic research enhances the efficiency of writing processes as well as boosts production and improves the quality of output. The research process can be broken down into two key components: coming up with a hypothesis and carrying out the study ([Pigg, 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib58)). For individuals new to the world of research, AI can help in both of these areas. When it comes to detecting deficiencies in the current body of literature, machines are better at finding patterns and connections than humans typically are especially when the human is just starting out ([Budhwar et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib13)). And once a hypothesis has been formed, AI can also assist with the next step: actually, carrying out the study.

Academic research benefits greatly from AI in data processing ([Alberth, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib3)). This is because AI can detect patterns and discover new things at an accelerated pace, that is it can spot the important stuff fast. And when we say “the important stuff,” we mean the critical elements for advancing research that invariably lead to new discoveries or revisions of previous theories ([Mahapatra, 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib46)). Quick and neat data organization and visualization are among the AI system's most useful tasks, with tables and figures forming the core of its talents ([Feher, 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib23)). That's because “comparable” is how we want our data to be side-by-side, in a way that allows us to see both their similarities and differences. And that's precisely how AI can help: It can find and highlight the patterns in our data that are easy to miss when we're working at human speed ([Gouveia et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib26)). The influence of AI is growing steadily and touching every aspect of daily existence ([Cheng & Lee, 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib16)). There are numerous comparisons drawn between the rapid growth of the internet in the 1990s and the current advancements in AI-based technology. The internet has significantly transformed our capacity to rapidly and effectively access and handle vast quantities of data however there is an important framework to follow while using generative AI in research writing as shown in [Fig. 7.2](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#fig2).

![image](images/f07-02.jpg)

Fig. 7.2. Framework for a Responsible Use of Generative AI. *Source:* Authors' own conceptualization.

As with any technical advancement, there is initial reluctance to adopt it due to worries over possible drawbacks or risks. Indeed, the extensive use of AI-driven technologies gives rise to legitimate concerns among certain individuals ([Bail, 2024](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib7)). Relying on outcomes generated by AI can hinder human creativity and analytical thinking abilities. Moreover, AI outcomes are influenced by biased or error-prone inputs.

The advent of generative AI has resulted in substantial changes to various domains within the academic realm. These improvements have been due to the ability to simplify language formation, customize learning experiences, improve content creation, and update academic research ([Alberth, 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib3)). Moreover, it has completely revolutionized the method of academic writing. Generative AI, often known as AI, has significantly improved the rationality and understanding of cognitive research ([Gouveia et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib26)). This is especially true for individuals who are not native English speakers and have previously faced challenges in briefly articulating their views. Additionally, creative AI enhances the ability of academics and students to effectively manage their time and improve reading skills by reducing long text and extracting important information ([Dogru et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib20)). Creative AI facilitates a customized learning environment within the realm of education, taking into account individual students' distinct learning preferences and styles. Furthermore, it enhances the quality and practicality of cognitive communication and improves linguistic experiences by developing language proficiency and correcting grammatical errors ([Wach et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib80)). The allocation of research projects has been transformed, leading to a radical shift in the nature of academic research.

The term “technology benefits” refers to the benefits derived from the use of a specific technology ([Dencik et al., 2023](#978-1-83608-298-920251007.xhtml#bib18)). The benefits of AI surround the benefits students receive from using technology in their academic endeavors. However, the term “actual technology use” refers to the diverse methods individuals employ technology in their daily routines.
